**BUILT HERE RATHER THAN FETCHED, AND THE ARITHMETIC IS THE WHOLE ARGUMENT.** #351's endpoint spent two operations a symbol — a session read and a tool item read — and this page has both in hand several lines earlier. Inlining costs **zero** operations: measured at 6 for a tool item with one log row, which is exactly #340's recorded `5 + one per 50 log rows` and unchanged by the symbol. The endpoint's second operation existed to refuse an id with no row, and the not-found return above has already refused it. So the page pays nothing and asks nothing twice, and the section above records what that did to the endpoint.

**IT IS DRAWN AT ITS PRINTED SIZE, WHICH IS THE ONE SIZE THAT NEEDED NO CHOOSING.** The alternative was to invent an on-screen size, and #353's own rule against that applies: a value chosen to make an undesigned screen look finished becomes the baseline a design has to argue with. The printed size is not invented — it is the same module size and the same per-symbol box the label screen uses, so **the screen becomes a preview of the physical object** and a reader can hold the worn sticker against it. Measured the same way on both screens: 21.089 mm at 0.57 mm a module on the tool item page, on the sheet with one label, and on all four labels of a four-label sheet. That equality is the issue's claim and it is now a measurement. **#412 re-sized the label and the equality held: 13.2 mm at 0.4 mm a module, on both screens.** **#431 stacked it and the module did not move, so neither did the symbol: 13.2 mm on both screens again.** **#453 took the headroom and the module still did not move: 13.2 mm on both screens, and a symbol a version larger 14.8 mm on the tool item page while the sheet refuses it.** **#467 put the module at 0.29 mm and the equality held: 9.57 mm on both screens, and a symbol a version larger 10.73 mm on the tool item page while the label screen refuses it.** **#457 ended the equality on screen and kept it on paper**: the page draws 9.568 mm, the dialog draws the same label at twice its size, 19.14 mm, and prints it at its own — so the page is still the preview of the physical object and the dialog's pane is not.

**THE SYMBOL IS NOT HERE TO BE SCANNED OFF THE SCREEN, AND SAYING SO IS WHAT SETTLES ITS SIZE.** A reader who typed the id because the sticker had worn through has already arrived; a scan of the screen would return them where they are. The scan that does work is Phase 3's, against a label on a tool. So the symbol carries **no legibility floor of its own** — unlike the readable id on the label, whose floor #353 set because it is a functional fallback — and nothing here invents one. Scanning the screen is not forbidden, merely not what the size answers to.

**PRINTING GOES THROUGH THE LABEL SCREEN AND THIS PAGE PRINTS NOTHING.** The control is a link carrying this one tool item. Two reasons, and the second is the stronger: the label screen owns the layout, the page box and the print rule, so a print path here would be a second implementation of one rule — and **printing one label is the archetypal part-used sheet**, which is exactly what #353's start position is for. Printing from here would either lose that control or rebuild it, and the app's most common print would become its worst sheet-waster. **#467 took the second reason with the sheet** — a label is a page of its own, so nothing is part used — and the first is the one that holds. It also makes "the same physical object as the original" true by construction rather than by comparison: there is no second layout to drift. `offline/tool-item-view.mjs` holds that this page names no stylesheet, no page box and no print call. **#457 made the control open the labels' dialog on the label this page built**, and the first reason holds of the dialog: it owns the page box and the print rule, and the page still names neither.

**NO SCREEN DRAWS THE `alt`, AND THIS PARAGRAPH SAID IT WAS THE FAILURE STATE UNTIL #455 LOOKED.** It refused an empty `alt` because the one way the image could fail was a session expiring between the render and the fetch — which is #351's endpoint, and #352 deleted that endpoint in the commit that added the string. The page inlines the symbol's markup, so nothing is fetched and nothing carries an `alt`: read in a browser, the page holds no `img`, and its one `svg` has no `role`, no `aria-label` and no `title`. `TOOL_ITEM_COPY.symbolAlt` has never been read under `app/`. `scripts/screen-strings.mjs` lists it all the same, because the page imports the whole constant — the fabricated string `docs/briefs/strings/unfindable.md` warns is believed where a missing one is caught — and that is how the design came to reword it; #455 took the word, since it is the design's. Whether the symbol should be named by it, or hidden because the heading already carries the id, is a decision this paragraph made on a false premise and nobody has made since, and the brief tells the design so. `docs/briefs/strings/unreachable.md` is not where it went: that file's subject is text that renders in principle, and this does not. Wherever it goes it stays out of the JSX: `offline/tool-list-view.mjs` fails an `alt` literal on this axis.

**THE CONTROL SAYS `Print the label` AND NOT `Reprint`, AND THE REASON IS THAT THE SCREEN CANNOT KNOW WHICH IT IS.** It said `Reprint this label` until this was read on a screen: the page is reached by somebody whose sticker has worn through, so a re-print is the case that motivated the control — but **nothing in this base records whether a sticker was ever printed or stuck on**, so a control promising one states what the app cannot check, and the reader printing a label for the first time meets a word that is wrong for them. The two want the same act. `offline/tool-label-page.mjs` pins the string and holds that no opener claims a re-print, since the word is the kind that comes back. **#457 took the design's `Print label`**, which is the dialog's title as well, and claims a re-print no more than this did.

**AND A LOG ENTRY'S MOMENT IS A DATE AND A TIME TO THE MINUTE, WHICH WAS THIS PAGE'S LAST UNRENDERED FACT.** It printed the stored value — a UTC instant to the millisecond, the most machine-shaped string on the page — and its brief had recorded the rendering as open since #340. The five parts are `/prs/[prId]`'s history's, the only other history in the app, so this followed rather than chose. **That made a fourth inline copy of those five options and the duplication was kept on purpose**: extracting one formatter would have edited two screens in another area to serve this one, so `EVENT_AT_FORMAT` was named here and `offline/tool-item-view.mjs` read the options off `/prs/[prId]`'s own call site and required the two to agree. The measurable condition for merging was a fifth site or the first screen wanting a different resolution. **#374 IS BOTH AT ONCE AND THE CONDITION FIRED** — it has to reach every site that renders an instant, which is what removes the reason to leave them apart, and `/prs/new`'s duplicate warning wants a day with no time on it. The options are `lib/format.js:INSTANT_FORMAT` now; the name went with them, `Event At` being one field on this table rather than a resolution.

**WHAT THAT COST WAS THE ZONE, AND #374 IS WHERE IT WAS PAID.** Both pages were Server Components, so `toLocaleString` resolved against wherever the render happened — UTC on Vercel — and the string no longer carried the `Z` the stored value had, so a reader on a site could not tell from the page which zone they were reading. **This axis is where that mattered most and the issue says so**: every other screen is read at a desk where the server's zone and the reader's are more often the same, and this is the one a scan opens on a phone. A `Tool Log` row's moment is drawn by `app/components/Instant.js` now, so `logRowFacts` hands the pair the stored instant and marks it — see that component's header for why the rendering has to cross a client boundary and what a reader sees before it does. **The check still cannot hold the rendering**: `toLocaleString` depends on the runtime's locale and timezone, so a pinned string would pass where it was written and fail in CI. What `offline/tool-item-view.mjs` pins is that exactly one of the four facts is a moment and that it is handed over unformatted; the options and the zone rule are `offline/instant-rendering.mjs`'s.

**AND AN UNPARSEABLE INSTANT COMES BACK UNCHANGED RATHER THAN AS `Invalid Date`.** A log row's four facts never drop, so there is always a pair to fill, and a stored string the formatter cannot read is more honestly shown as itself than as the formatter's complaint. A blank stays blank. **The rule generalized with the formatter in #374** — it holds on every screen now, for the reason it held here.

**AND THERE IS NO TOOL ITEM WHOSE LABEL IS MISSING**, which looks like a state and is not. A symbol is a pure function of the id and the host, so every tool item that exists has one; what can be missing is a sticker on the drill, and nothing in this base records whether one was printed or stuck on. The page therefore has two states rather than three — a tool item with its symbol, or the not-found refusal with neither symbol nor print control.

## The two list screens, and the app's first paging (#339)

`/tools` is every tool with a count per status. `/tools/[toolRecordId]` is one tool's tool items, twenty-five at a time since #442 and ten before it — **it stood at `/tools/tool/[toolRecordId]` until #348**. `lib/toolListView.js` holds both screens' rules and every word either says.

**THE TOOL'S SCREEN WENT A LEVEL DEEPER RATHER THAN INTO A QUERY PARAMETER, AND THE DEPTH IS THE ONLY HALF #348 UNDID.** The extra segment was forced by the flat slot being spent; the argument against a parameter was not, and it is why this is still a route rather than a state of `/tools`. #336 and #340 left the choice open and named both options. `offline/url-parameters.mjs` inventories every parameter the app carries, and before this issue they were three kinds: a filter narrowing which rows appear, a navigation opening a form on a record, and a one-time account of something the arrival does not say. **None of them changes what the page is a list of** — `/materials?q=` searched still lists materials. `/tools?tool=rec…` would have made one route render two different tables, which no screen in this app does and which the brief system cannot describe: a brief is one file per page with a section for what the page carries only sometimes, and neither of those two lists is the other's exception. Against that, every list-to-one-record pair in this app is a route rather than a state, five for five. So the tool's screen is a route, `page` is the axis's first parameter, and it is a fourth group in that inventory — a SLICE. A filter is a question and a page is an answer too long to hand over at once.

**THE SEGMENT IS AIRTABLE'S RECORD ID, WHICH IS `/materials/[materialId]`'s ANSWER TO THE SAME PROBLEM.** `Tools` mints no id for the reason above — nothing prints a tool — and the primary field is a name a person typed. A name is not a path: it can hold any character, and the first spelling wins only because nothing renames a tool yet, so a route built on it would move the day something does. Giving `Tools` a minted ID was the other option and is refused: its only purpose would be a prettier URL, against a decision this file already records with its reasons.

**BUT THE PARAMETER IS `toolRecordId` AND NOT `toolId`, WHICH IS WHERE THIS DEPARTS FROM THE PRECEDENT IT FOLLOWS.** There is no `Tool ID` field and there is no `Material ID` field either — `/materials/[materialId]` names its segment after a field that does not exist, which is exactly the failure `naming.md` opens by describing: somebody reads the identifier, searches the base for it, and finds nothing. Following the shape without repeating the defect costs one word. The row is in `naming.md`'s table.

**THE COUNT PER STATUS IS THE FLATTENED LINK ARRAYS, AND THE TRIGGER FOR CHANGING THAT IS NOW A NUMBER.** `getAllTools` returns every tool carrying its `Tool Items` array; flattening those and reading them through `getToolItemsByRecordIds` is one query per 50, so `/tools` costs **`2 + ceil(total tool items / 50)`** — measured at 3 on this base's 18. The condition this file already stated for moving the count into a rollup was "above roughly ten operations", which is **401 tool items**; the figure is written down because a prose threshold is one nobody can act on without re-deriving it. Nothing about the number of TOOLS drives it: `getAllTools` is one query per 100 rows, and a catalog is tens.

**ALL THREE STATUSES ON EVERY ROW, A ZERO INCLUDED, AND THE ROW IS BUILT FROM `TOOL_STATUS_VALUES`.** An absent `Out` reads as "not known" where `0` reads as "none out", which is the nothing-against-zero distinction `_shared.md` already draws. Deriving the row from the vocabulary rather than writing the three out is what makes a fourth status impossible to add silently — it would appear on the screen without anyone editing the module, and `offline/tool-list-view.mjs` asserts the two agree.

**NO TOTAL PER TOOL, AND THE REASON IS `Retired` RATHER THAN THE ISSUE'S WORDING.** A single figure would have to count a retired tool or not, and both readings are wanted: what the company holds now, and what it has ever bought. Three counts answer both without choosing. The tool's own screen DOES state a single total, and that is not the same figure — it is how many rows the list has, which is what #326 names as the fact every list in this app is missing, and it is free here because the link array carries its own length.

**THE PAGING DIVIDES THE READ AS WELL AS THE RENDER, AND #326 CANNOT.** That issue's body settles the document lists' shape and states the limit: most of those lists admit rows through `canViewPR`, whose ordered rules are not expressible as a query, so a page of records read from the base is not a page of rows a reader sees and the count would differ per reader. **Nothing gates a tool item per row (#337)**, so here the page is chosen from the parent's link array before anything is fetched and only that slice is read — the tool's screen is four operations whatever the tool's size. **It stays four only while a page is inside one read of fifty and the read is handed the page**, and #442 is where the second of those got a check. That is a property of the axis rather than a preference, which is why #326 inherits the shape (the page number in the URL, one query per page, a screen that says which page it is) and not this half.

**THE SIZE IS TEN AND IT IS THE ONE NUMBER ON THIS AXIS WITH NO MEASUREMENT BEHIND IT.** The batch does not decide it — any size up to 50 costs exactly one `findChildRecords` query — so it is chosen for the reader: three short facts per row, ten rows, a screenful at the 375px this axis is used at. Nobody has held the list on a phone with a real warehouse in it. It is stated in `docs/briefs/tools-toolRecordId.md` as a fact whose rendering is open, **because a number that lives only in a module is one the design work cannot see and therefore cannot re-decide**. This cited #340's raw timestamp as the parallel case; **that one was settled in #352** and this is now the only open figure of the two, which is the mechanism working rather than the parallel failing. Whether it and #326's size should be one number is that issue's to settle: these screens assume a phone and the document lists assume a monitor. **#442 MADE IT TWENTY-FIVE, THE DESIGN'S FIGURE FOR THE LIST AS IT DREW IT**, which is the mechanism this paragraph describes doing what it was for; and #326 settled the last question itself — the two sizes stay apart — which `The page the design settled on` carries forward now that both are 25.

**OLDEST FIRST, WHICH IS THE LINK ARRAY'S ORDER AND IS LOAD-BEARING UNDER PAGING.** A link array is creation order, and `createToolItems` mints contiguous ids under one lock, so it is also ascending `Tool Item ID` — the number a person reads off a label. `findChildRecords` preserves it and nothing sorts, which is #340's arrangement one screen over. Newest first is wrong for a paged list specifically: every registration would push the rows along, so a link to page 2 would name different tool items tomorrow. **#463 turned it newest first, the design's order (1b), and accepted that cost**: a link to a later page names other tool items after a registration, and the selection, being printed ids, moves with nothing.

**A PAGE NOBODY CAN BE ON RESOLVES TO ONE THEY CAN.** A URL is typed, edited and copied, so `?page=` is a request rather than a promise; `pageOfToolItems` clamps and slices in one function so no call site can take a page without having resolved which page it is, which is `readQuantity`'s shape (#338) for the same reason. A tool with nothing under it has one page, which is the empty state rather than page 0 of 0.

**A TOOL WITH NO TOOL ITEMS IS REACHABLE AND THE SCREEN SAYS SO.** Registration writes the `Tools` row first and creates the tool items after it, and #338 rolls back neither — so a failure in between leaves a tool standing alone. It reads as three zeros on the list and as a sentence on the tool's own screen, said as what happened rather than as an error, the way the tool item page says its missing history. No such row is on the base today.

**WHAT A GREEN RUN DOES NOT PROVE HERE, BEYOND THE TIER'S STANDING LIMIT.** Every tool item on this base is `In Stock` and no screen in this app writes a `Checked Out`, `Checked In` or `Retired` row — registration is the only event that exists — so **`Out` and `Retired` have never been seen with a figure in them**. They are drawn from the vocabulary, so nothing about the screen changes when they are; what has not been observed is the rendering. **Tools · Phase 3 — Check-out & Check-in is where the first such row gets written, and both of these screens are a verification target on it** — the list for a count that is not zero, the tool's own page for a `Status` column holding more than one value. **#362 and #363 are where this stopped being true**: the app writes all three events now, and both screens have rendered all three statuses with figures in them.

**`offline/tool-list-view.mjs` ALSO HOLDS SOMETHING WIDER THAN THIS ISSUE.** #338 and #340 both state in prose that every string a tools screen renders is in a constant and none is in JSX — so a vocabulary sweep and `scripts/screen-strings.mjs` can reach it — and nothing held it. The check reads every file under `app/(tools)/` and fails on bare JSX text, on a string literal in an expression container, and on a visible attribute. **`/tools` was in fact carrying one**, `<h1>Tools</h1>` from #336, which is what a rule with no check is for.

**CLAUDE.md's THREE TOOLS SERVICE-LAYER LINES WERE TRIMMED TO PAY FOR THE FOURTH.** The file had 345 bytes of headroom and this issue needed a line. #334's test is the one applied: a clause whose readers are exactly the glob the index already routes belongs in the file it routes to. All three carried bolded rules that bind only tools code and are here already — and `lib/toolStatus.js`'s was a straight duplicate of the sentence the `Tool Items` data-model entry carries in the same file, twenty lines down. `Notes` being the fact that drops was the only clause with no home in this file, and it has one now. Trimming was preferred to moving the `Units` section, which had been named as the next candidate and fails that test: its readers are five tables across four areas, so no index row sends them anywhere.

### The page the design settled on (#442)

A tool's list holds **twenty-five** tool items to a page, from ten. The design drew the list and set the figure; `TOOL_PAGE_SIZE` carries it, and nothing else about the paging moved.

**A DESIGN'S FIGURE, NOT A MEASUREMENT, AND THE DESIGN'S TO MOVE AGAIN.** Ten was an estimate of a screenful of three short facts at 375px, made before any screen was drawn. Twenty-five replaces it with a decision rather than with evidence — nobody has yet held this list on a phone with a real warehouse in it — so the brief states it as the design's value for the list as drawn, which moves if the list is drawn again. **What is not the design's is the ceiling, and there are two.** A page up to fifty costs one read and a fifty-first row costs a second, and a page is how many labels one press of print asks for, which the label screen printed a hundred at a time and the labels' dialog reads a hundred at most of (#457).

**STILL FOUR OPERATIONS, MEASURED ON THE LARGEST PAGE THIS BASE CAN PRODUCE.** On a dev server against the live base: at ten, the largest tool's first page (ten rows) and second (two) were 4 each; at twenty-five, the same tool on one page of twelve is 4 — the session, the tool, one `Tool Items` read and the job list. **A page of twenty-five cannot be produced from existing rows**: the base holds eighteen tool items, twelve under the largest tool. What carries the claim past twelve is `findChildRecords`, which reads fifty ids a query (`BATCH_CHUNK` in `lib/airtable/client.js`). **That a fifty-first row adds a step was already written** — in the check's own bound and its comment, in `TOOL_PAGE_SIZE`'s docstring and in `THE SIZE IS TEN` above — so nothing new was needed for it.

**THE PRINT'S SCOPE IS WHERE THE FIGURE STOPPED BEING ONLY A LAYOUT VALUE, AND THAT IS WHY THE RELATION IS WRITTEN NOW.** The tool's page sends the label screen the tool items on the page it shows, and the label screen prints `MAX_LABELS_PER_REQUEST` at a time — a cap tied to the registration form by import, and tied to this page by nothing. At ten against a hundred there was nothing to say; at twenty-five the two figures meet the same person in the same flow, and a cut to the registration cap below a page would make a press that prints part of its page. So both docstrings state the order, and `offline/tool-list-view.mjs` holds it beside the literal it pins. **#443 changed both halves of the premise and the order still binds**: the page sends its selection rather than the page, `lib/toolListView.js` now imports the cap to refuse a selection it cannot print, and what a page larger than the cap would break is the page box's own first press.

**THE ARGUMENT BOTH CLAIMS REST ON HAD NOTHING HOLDING IT, AND THAT WAS THE ISSUE'S MOST VALUABLE FINDING.** The four operations and a press printing one page are both true only because the page hands `getToolItemsByTool` `rowIds: page.ids` and builds its print link from what that read returned. Drop the argument and the page reads every tool item under the tool and prints every one of them — and no figure on the screen moves, so neither a value check nor a browser glance would notice. The check reads the call's argument and the link's source off the AST now, beside a planted screen that does both wrong. **Since #443 the second claim is the page box's**: the print link is the list's, built from the selection, and what the check reads on the page is the rows it hands the list. **Since #457 the link is the dialog's opener**, handed the same selection.

**THREE 25s IN THIS APP NOW, AND THE WARNING WAS MADE EXECUTABLE RATHER THAN ONLY LOUDER.** `LIST_PAGE_SIZE` and `/materials`' search bound were already 25; `docs/notes/deliveries-and-invoices.md` recorded that two of them matched and warned against folding. With all three equal the incentive is at its strongest, and the likeliest hand is #258's, whose task is to name each design value once — two equal page sizes in two files is the first thing it would reach for. Prose alone was judged not enough: the warning now sits above BOTH declarations, so folding either means editing the line under it, and the check fails the two page sizes unless each is declared as a number of its own in a module that does not reach the other's — a shared token, an alias and a re-export all fail. **The one legitimate merge is a design decision that the two are one reader's page**, taken where #258 names the design's values, with the check and both docstrings changed in that commit. The values themselves are not pinned by that part of the check: this one is pinned beside it, and the document lists derive theirs on purpose. The materials bound is named in prose only — it is not a page at all.

**THE BRIEF'S RARE-CASE SENTENCE BECAME A CONDITION, AND WHAT IT WAS FOR GOES TO THE DESIGN SEPARATELY.** It read "a second page is the rare case … one purchase is at most fifty tools and usually a single digit", and it was wrong twice: one registration can make a hundred, and the list under a tool sums every registration of it rather than one purchase. It also stated a frequency, which `docs/briefs/_shared.md` rules out for any brief. So it now says only when a second page exists — past twenty-five tool items under one tool, counted across its registrations. **The sentence was there for a reason the condition does not carry**: it was written at #348 so the design would not put the paging controls at the center of the screen. That guidance is handed to the design directly rather than restated in the brief as a frequency.

**THE CHECK PINS THE FIGURE NOW, AND THE MULTI-PAGE FIXTURE HAD TO CHANGE WITH IT.** Every length in the paging loop was derived from the constant, and the only thing that held the size was a twelve-row fixture — by the accident of twelve rows being two pages at ten. Measured: the check as it stood fails five of its 74 at twenty-five, all five of them that fixture's page-2 assertions, and a version of them bent to pass would be asking about a list with no second page. So the size is pinned at 25, the loop runs literal lengths against literal page counts across both edges a page of twenty-five has inside a hundred, and the fixture is fifty-two rows — three pages with a middle one — whose page count is asserted first. **Fourteen mutations were run and all fourteen fail it**; seven of them pass every figure the file asserts — the read losing its argument or taking the whole array, the print link built from the tool, the label cap cut under a page, and the three ways of folding the two page sizes — and fail only on the source or on the relation.

**WHAT A BROWSER COULD SHOW AND WHAT IT COULD NOT.** At 1440 and at 375 the largest tool's twelve rows sit on one page reading `Page 1 of 1`, with neither step, and its print link carries the same twelve ids in the same order; `?page=2` lands on that page. **No page boundary can be seen on this base**, since no tool has more than twenty-five tool items, so the boundary is what the fixture holds and nothing more.

### Which tool items a label run prints (#443)

A tool's list carries a box on every entry and one for the page, a sentence saying what is selected, a way to clear it, and a print control that sends exactly the selection. The rules and the words are `lib/toolListView.js`, the address is `lib/toolRoutes.js` — `toolPath` writes the selection and `readToolItemIds` reads it, for this screen and the label screen alike, and since #457 for the labels' read — and the list is `app/(tools)/tools/[toolRecordId]/ToolItemList.js`, whose header carries the decision a later reader is likeliest to undo.

**THE SECOND ROW SELECTION IN THE APP, NOT THE FIRST.** The label screen's per-label include (#353) was the first, and this one is built to agree with it: the same values under the same name, handed from one screen to the next unchanged. The grounds for bringing the pattern to a list were two, and both are the issue's: the range a press prints is on the screen as a state rather than in a sentence, and printing part of a page becomes possible — three stickers spoiled on a sheet is the case. **#457 took the include with its screen**, so this is the one selection a run passes through.

**THE SELECTION IS PRINTED IDS UNDER THE LABEL SCREEN'S OWN NAME, `id`.** One set named twice is two things to whoever reads only one address, so the list's parameter is the one the print control lands on. Record ids were weighed, since the tool's link array holds them and would have let the page check a selection against the tool for free — and refused, because the label screen takes printed ids: turning an off-page record id into one is a read, and the alternative, a second input on the label screen, names one run two ways. **In the inventory it joins the fifth group rather than founding a seventh.** The groups are sorted by what a reload does, and both members answer the same: the same records come back named for the same act. Of the four exclusions that group rests on, only "not a filter" changes its reason — the list renders the same rows with or without the parameter, so it marks them rather than narrowing them. **Since #457 no screen lands on it**: the labels are a dialog whose read takes the ids as the list hands them, so `id` is this page's alone, and the label screen's half of the group went with that screen.

**NO SHORTHAND FOR A WHOLE PAGE, AND THE REASON IS THAT A PAGE'S EDGES MOVE.** `page:2` or `all-on-page` would be bound to where a page begins and ends, which moves when the design moves `TOOL_PAGE_SIZE` — #442 just did — and when a registration grows the list, the last page then and every page since #463, so a copied address would name different tool items later with nothing to say so. Positions in the link array do not move, being append-only, but they are unreadable in an address and meet the record-id objection above. The label code is seven characters shorter and is the sticker's own string (#411), which nothing but `labelCodeFor` makes. **So the address is explicit ids, measured at 21 bytes each**: 524 for a full page, 2,099 at the most one print takes. A selection can grow past what prints, and Node's default header limit — 16,384 bytes, read locally off `http.maxHeaderSize` — would refuse a request at roughly seven hundred; nothing has come near it, and the list's header records the remedy rather than building it.

**THE PAGE BOX REACHES THIS PAGE, WHICH IS THE PAGING'S CONSEQUENCE AND IS SAID THREE WAYS.** Selecting the whole tool needs the printed ids of tool items this render never read — the read #339 divided, and the same reason the brief bars printing all of a tool. The screen shows the scope in the box's own words (`Select all on this page`), in its state — none, some or all of this page, whatever is selected elsewhere — and in the sentence's second clause, `…, 1 not on this page`, which appears only when it is not zero and is what reconciles a count with a page whose boxes show fewer. **The list is handed only the rows the page read**, so it cannot select past them by construction, and the check reads the rows' source off the page.

**NOTHING SELECTED PRINTS NOTHING, AND THAT IS THE ISSUE RATHER THAN A SIDE OF IT.** The print control sent the page it was on and had to say so in words; keeping that as the default for an empty selection would put the range back off the screen, and the control would need two wordings for two scopes. The page is one press of the page box away and that press shows it — on every tool this base holds, one press is the whole tool. The sentence is the label screen's own for the same state, so the two screens a run passes through refuse one empty selection in one voice. **#457 moved it into `TOOL_LIST_COPY`**: the dialog never opens on an empty selection, so the list is the one place that refuses one. The control stays drawn, because it is what says the boxes are for printing; `Clear selection` is absent with nothing to clear, as the steps are at their ends. **Clear earns its place where the label screen's select-none did not (#376's reasoning, run again)**: that selection starts full on every visit, while this one outlives a page turn and a reload, so it needs an exit that does not mean visiting every page.

**A SELECTION LARGER THAN ONE PRINT DOES NOT PRINT.** The label screen takes the first hundred of a longer address and says so, which would make a press print less than it sent — and that screen's brief records that nothing the app produces reaches that state, which stays true only because this refuses first. No tool on this base can reach it: it needs more than a hundred tool items under one tool, selected across pages. **Since #457 the read behind the labels' dialog throws on a longer run instead**, so this is the refusal a reader meets and the throw is for a caller with no page.

**THE LABEL SCREEN KEEPS ITS INCLUDE, AND THE TWO ARE NOT ONE CONTROL.** The list assembles a run across pages and carries it in the address; the include trims a run already named, for this one print — beside the start position and the sheet count until #467, and the host warning until #454 — and writes nothing back. It is also the only place the whole of a cross-page selection is listed at once, and the only selection a tool item's page arrives with; a registration's arrived from its own answer until #449, and arrives through the tool's page now. Two acts, two words: `Select` there, `Include` here. **#457 took the include with the screen**: the dialog asks nothing, so the selection is the run, and the dialog's pane is where a cross-page selection is drawn at once.

**READ ON THE CLIENT, WHICH IS THE OPPOSITE OF #373 AND HAD TO BE ARGUED.** `/login` reads its destination on the server and hands a prop down, and the next reader will reach for that. Here a press rewrites the address with `history.replaceState` and renders nothing, so a server-read prop describes the address the page was rendered for — and Next 16.2.10 keeps that render: its patched `replaceState` copies the current entry's tree into the replacing one and restores it, and Back restores the tree stored in the entry. **Measured in a browser, with the page's reading handed down as a temporary probe and removed afterwards**: two boxes pressed, a tool item opened, Back — no request reached the server, and the probe still read no selection while the address and the boxes carried two. `router.replace` would keep a server reader honest by fetching again, which is one render and four operations per press. The component's header carries the whole of this, because that is where somebody following #373 will be standing.

**KEPT IN THE LIST'S ORDER, WHICH A BROWSER DECIDED.** It was kept in the order made when first built, and selecting three and then the page put those three at the head of the sheet and the other nine after them — the order a person working down the list to the tools would not expect. Every press now leaves it oldest first, by the id's day and then its sequence as a number through `lib/idSequence.js:sequenceOf`, since `-1000` sorts as text ahead of `-999`; that is #40's reason for the same call. One selection is therefore one address whatever order its boxes were pressed in. A hand-written address keeps its own order until its first press, which only a person typing one can meet. **#463 turned the list newest first and left this as it was**: a press still leaves the selection in ascending id, the list's order no longer, so a run prints as its numbers count up and a person working down the list meets its stickers the other way round.

**THE FIGURES, ON A DEV SERVER AGAINST THE LIVE BASE.** The tool's page is **4 operations** with no selection, with three selected, with the whole page selected, and with an address naming two of another tool's tool items — the server never reads the parameter. Pressing a box sends **no request**: across twenty presses — fifteen at 1440, five at 375, boxes, the page box and Clear — the server received nothing for the page and printed no scope, and every request in its log is a navigation, a reload or a hot reload after an edit. The label screen is **3**, unchanged. The count and the not-on-this-page figure come from the address and the rows already drawn.

**WHAT THE CHECK HOLDS, AND TWO THINGS ONLY A MUTATION FOUND.** The rules are asserted by value, at literal sizes either side of the cap, with each input's distinct count asserted before anything is asked of it; where the selection lives — the page reading only `page`, the rows it hands on, the print link and both steps built from the selection, a history write and no router — is read off both files' ASTs beside planted screens doing each wrong. **Twenty-five mutations were run and all twenty-five fail the tier.** One passed first, measured by putting the check back as it was: reading `id` with `get` rather than `getAll` — one selected where three were — survived every value and structural assertion until the argument itself was asserted. The not-on-this-page case was also given two entries on the page and one off, on the reasoning that one of each would hide the two counts swapped; measured the same way, that swap was already caught by the one-selected case, so the change is a second path to the claim rather than a repair.

**WHAT A BROWSER COULD SHOW AND WHAT IT COULD NOT.** At 1440 and at 375: a press rewrote the address and nothing else; a reload kept the selection; the page box went from some to all to none; Clear emptied it; Print opened the label screen listing exactly the selection, in the list's order; Back returned to it intact; and at 375 nothing scrolled sideways. **A page turn could not be seen**, since no tool on this base has more than twenty-five tool items — an address naming another tool's tool items stood in for a selection off the page, and `toolPath` carrying the selection on both steps is held by the check. The refusal past a hundred could not be seen either.

**THE WORDS NAME NO NOUN FOR WHAT IS SELECTED.** `tool item` is decided against appearing with its replacement undecided (#378, #376), and a bare `item` names a row of four other tables, so the selection says `3 selected` — the document lists' pickers' shape (#324) — and the sweep that settles the word has nothing here to undo. **#455 was that sweep and it had nothing here to undo**: the design's word on this page is `item` — `27 items`, the `Item` column — and the selection's words still name none.

## The four addresses, and the one a label prints (#348)

`/tools` is the tool list, `/tools/[toolRecordId]` is one tool, `/tool-items/[toolItemId]` is one tool item, and `/t/[labelCode]` is what a label encodes and redirects to the third. The screens sit in the `app/(tools)/` route group so one layout still holds the axis's width; the address a label prints sat there too until #478 took it out, to the root layout alone, at the same address (`The account at the rail's foot`). `lib/toolRoutes.js` holds every one of those addresses; `offline/tool-routes.mjs` holds it to what the app serves.

**THE CONDITION FOR REACHING FOR A ROUTE GROUP, WHICH IS THE USEFUL FORM OF THIS.** A route group buys one thing: a layout over a set of routes that do not share a URL prefix. While every screen on an axis hangs off one segment, the directory that names the segment already is that set, and a group adds a level of nesting and a hole in every derivation that reads a path — so it is not free and there is nothing to buy. **Reach for one when a second top-level collection joins the axis**, which is what happened here: `/tool-items` and `/t` are not under `/tools` and must not be, and all three wanted the layout #336 put the width in. That is the whole test, and it is why this arrived at #348 rather than at #336. **`/t` stopped wanting it at #478**: a page that draws nothing has no use for the rail the layout draws, so it left the group, and since a group is not a segment its address did not move.

**WHAT THE GROUP COST, AND IT IS ONE FUNCTION PLUS FOUR READERS.** `routeTemplate` derived a route by stripping the `app` prefix, which is exact only while every directory is a URL segment. A parenthesized directory is not, so it strips those too now. Four files take that function's word for what a route is and each wanted something different from the answer:

- `offline/airtable-ops.mjs` derives the ops label a page must open its scope with. Nothing in the check changed; the pages now write `/tool-items/[toolItemId]` and `/t/[labelCode]` — `/t/[toolItemId]` until #411 — and a label carrying `(tools)` would have failed the comparison — which is the check working, since the label is a bucket a human reads.
- `offline/screen-briefs.mjs` derives a brief's FILENAME from the route, so the group is what keeps `tools-toolRecordId.md` from becoming `tools-tools-toolRecordId.md`. Two briefs were renamed and one added by the routes moving, and none by the group.
- `offline/url-parameters.mjs` attributes a parameter to a route, both from a page's own path and by walking up from a component. Its `CARRIED` row moved with the route.
- `scripts/screen-strings.mjs` needed the INVERSE, and it is the one that actually broke. It rebuilt a directory as `"app" + route`, which pointed at `app/tools` for a page living in `app/(tools)/tools` — every tools screen would have reported no strings at all, silently, since a screen with no strings is a legitimate answer. It maps a route back through the page files now, so the two directions cannot disagree.

CLAUDE.md's index glob moved to `app/(tools)/**` in the same commit, because `offline/notes-index.mjs` fails a row matching nothing — which is the mechanism catching the edit rather than a courtesy.

**`/t/` REDIRECTS RATHER THAN RENDERING, BECAUSE ONE PAGE HAS ONE ADDRESS.** A rewrite would have served the tool item's page under the short path at no hop at all, and it would have made two addresses for one screen — the thing #340's canonical redirect exists to prevent one level down. So `/t/` is an entry point that leaves.

**AND IT IS `redirect` (307) WHERE #340 IS `permanentRedirect` (308), WHICH IS A DIFFERENCE IN WHAT IS BEING MAPPED.** #340 maps a VARIANT of an address onto that address: a property of the id, true for as long as the id exists, so a 308 cached in a browser forever is correct. `/t/` maps a printed ENTRY POINT onto a SCREEN, and the screen's address is the app's to move — that separation is the entire reason this route exists. A permanent redirect would weld the two back together inside every scanner's cache, where nothing shipped later can reach them, and the app would have bought the freedom and then given it away. The cost is a round trip per scan.

**THE CASE IS NORMALIZED AT `/t/` AND THE RULE IS THAT A SCAN NEVER REDIRECTS TWICE.** The tool item's page redirects when the id it was handed is not the stored spelling, so handing it a typed `hye-tl-…` would cost a second hop on the one path that is measured in gloved hands. `canonicalToolItemId` trims and uppercases with no read behind it, which is sound because every `Tool Item ID` is minted uppercase — asserted against `formatSequentialId` rather than assumed, so the two cannot drift. The two are LAYERS rather than two rules: a cheap string transform in front, and the stored-value comparison behind it as the authority on what exists. Direct traffic to `/tool-items/…` still meets the second, at one hop.

### What the QR carries, calculated — and then measured (#351)

**These were character counts and capacity-table lookups when #348 wrote them, and #351 encoded a real address against them. Two survived and one was wrong; the section below this one carries the measurements and is the authority where the two disagree.** The host was `hyeusa.com`, and is `app.hyeusa.com` since #467 measured the address there. **#411 then shortened the segment itself, so every figure in this table is one issue out of date** — `What the label drops, and what that bought` carries what the address is now.

| What the code encodes | Characters |
|---|---|
| `https://hyeusa.com/t/HYE-TL-260909-004` | 38 |
| `https://hyeusa.com/tools/HYE-TL-260909-004`, the address before this issue | 42 |
| `https://hyeusa.com/tool-items/HYE-TL-260909-004`, had the screen kept the printed job | 47 |
| `https://hyeusa.com/t/260909-004`, the address since #411 | 31 |

At byte mode and error correction M, a version 3 symbol holds 42 bytes and version 4 holds 62 — so the third row is what this issue avoided rather than the second. **The lever that actually pays is the character set.** A QR code's alphanumeric mode holds digits, capital letters and `$ % * + - . / :` and packs them far tighter than byte mode, and `HTTPS://HYEUSA.COM/T/HYE-TL-260909-004` is entirely inside it. Against the published capacity table that is one version smaller at every error-correction level — at M, 38 alphanumeric characters fit version 2 (25 modules a side) where 38 bytes need version 3 (29). At a fixed sticker size that is a module about a sixth larger.

**SO THE LABEL PRINTS THE URL IN CAPITALS, AND `/T/` RESOLVES.** The scheme and host are case-insensitive per RFC 3986 and the path is not, so the uppercase spelling needs an answer of its own. It is a rewrite in `next.config.mjs` rather than a second route: **two directories differing only in case cannot coexist** — measured on this machine, `mkdir T` beside `t` was refused with `File exists` — and even where they could, two pages for one entry point would be two briefs and two entry points. A redirect was the other option and is refused for the reason above: it would make an uppercase scan cost two hops.

**TWO THINGS PHASE 2 STILL DECIDES, AND NEITHER IS THE ENCODING.** What the label prints beside the code — the readable id, a tool name, a job — and the physical size and error-correction level, which is where the version arithmetic above gets used. **What it does not have to re-open is the address or the character set.** **THE ERROR-CORRECTION LEVEL LEFT THAT LIST IN #351** and is settled at M, measured rather than derived from the arithmetic; the physical size is #353's alone, and what it gets handed is a module count. **#431 settled the first of the two: the label prints the symbol and the readable code under it, and neither a tool name nor a job.**

**AND THE SEQUENCE IS NOT CAPPED AT THREE DIGITS.** `nextSequence` widens past its pad rather than wrapping, so the thousandth tool item of one day is `HYE-TL-260909-1000` and every figure above is one character longer. **THIS SENTENCE SAID THAT WAS INSIDE VERSION 2 AT M WITH TWO CHARACTERS TO SPARE, AND IT IS NOT (#351).** Measured: version 2 at M holds exactly 38 alphanumeric characters, the 38-character address uses all of them, and 39 steps to version 3. Where the two characters were read from is not recoverable and is not worth guessing at; what matters is that the address sat exactly on the boundary, which the next section records with what it cost. **#411 IS WHERE THAT STOPPED BEING TRUE AND THE FOUR-DIGIT SEQUENCE BECAME AFFORDABLE** — see `What the label drops, and what that bought` below.

### The symbol itself, encoded rather than looked up (#351)

`GET /api/tool-items/[toolItemId]/qr` returns one tool item's QR symbol as SVG. `lib/toolLabelQR.js` is the pure half — the address, the level, the quiet zone and the figures — and `offline/tool-label-qr.mjs` is what encodes and reads the answers back.

**THIS IS THE FIRST ISSUE ON THE AXIS WHOSE NUMBERS ARE MEASUREMENTS, AND ONE OF THEM CAME BACK DIFFERENT.** The section above was capacity-table arithmetic. Encoding the real address confirmed two of its claims and broke a third: the route move to `/t/` does buy a version at M (42 characters needs version 3), the uppercase spelling does buy a version (the same 38 characters in lowercase segment into `Byte:20 + Alphanumeric:18` and need version 3), and **the four-digit sequence does not fit with two characters to spare.** Both figures were produced by two independent encoders — `qrcode` and `qrcode-generator` agree on every row — so the disagreement is with the earlier reading rather than with one library.

| Error correction | Version | Modules a side | What version 2 holds at that level |
|---|---|---|---|
| L, 7% | 2 | 25 | 47 alphanumeric characters |
| **M, 15%** | **2** | **25** | **38 — and the address is 38** |
| Q, 25% | 3 | 29 | 29 |
| H, 30% | 4 | 33 | 20 |

**THE VERSION COLUMN IS #351's ADDRESS AND #411 MOVED ONE ROW OF IT.** At 31 characters `H` produces version 3 rather than 4, so the highest level now costs exactly what `Q` costs — one version, 29 modules a side, 16% thinner at a fixed sticker size. The choice does not reopen and the paragraph below is why: what decided it was the finder pattern, which no address length touches.

**M, AND THE DECIDING MEASUREMENT IS NOT THE PERCENTAGE.** L and M produce the same version and the same 25 modules, so the level is free in printed density — and a solid blot painted over the data region kills L at 5x5 while M still decodes at 7x7. That is the whole argument for taking M over L. The argument against Q and H is the other measurement: **one flipped module inside a finder pattern leaves no symbol to find at L, M, Q and H alike.** Error correction protects the data region and does nothing for the corners, which is where a sticker peels and abrades first — so paying a version for Q or H buys recovery in the region that was not the failure mode and loses module width, which is what a gloved hand at arm's length actually depends on. The issue said to choose against a worn label rather than against capacity; this is that choice, and the cost was the zero headroom in the table. **#411 removed that cost and cut H's price in half, and the conclusion is unmoved** — which is the useful shape of this paragraph rather than a coincidence: a decision resting on the measurement that actually decided it survives the arithmetic around it changing.

**THE VERSION IS RECORDED AND NOT PASSED TO THE ENCODER, WHICH IS THE ONE DECISION HERE THAT COULD REASONABLY HAVE GONE THE OTHER WAY.** Passing `version: 2` makes the encoder refuse an address that no longer fits, which sounds like the safe direction: every printed symbol would then be exactly the geometry #353 measured. It is refused because the address is not fixed — the thousandth tool item of one day is one character longer and needs version 3 — so the pin turns a benign geometry change into a tool that cannot be labeled at all, and a row nothing can reach is what registration exists to prevent. So the encoder chooses, `buildToolItemQR` reports what it chose, and the pin lives in CI: `offline/tool-label-qr.mjs` encodes the production host — `https://app.hyeusa.com` since #467, `https://hyeusa.com` before — and fails if the answer is not version 2 at 25 modules. A drift in the address, the host, the level or the library is a failing check, which is earlier and louder than a runtime refusal. **Both halves of that reason have moved since** — #411 put the thousandth tool item inside version 2, and since #453 a version step is refused by the labels' dialog anyway — so what not pinning buys now is where the refusal lands, which `lib/toolLabelQR.js`' header carries.

**WHICH MAKES ONE THING #353'S OBLIGATION RATHER THAN A PREFERENCE: SIZE BY THE MODULE, NOT BY THE SYMBOL.** A version step adds four modules to a side, so a fixed-width box renders them 16% thinner while a per-module width renders a larger label. Only the second stays scannable. What this issue hands over is therefore a count and not a millimeter: **25 modules of symbol, 4 of quiet zone on each side, 33 on a side of the `viewBox`** — and the SVG carries no `width` or `height` at all, so a consumer cannot accidentally inherit a size. `QR_SIDE_MODULES` is a constant as well as a value on the builder's return, because a number reachable only through an async call is one a label layout cannot read while it is being drawn.

**THE QUIET ZONE IS INSIDE THE SVG, AND NO CHECK IN THIS REPOSITORY CAN JUSTIFY IT.** The `viewBox` covers the symbol plus four modules on each side and the renderer paints white across the whole box, so a colored label stock or a mark printed beside it cannot bleed into the margin — and #353's obligation collapses to "do not print over this box". What cannot be shown is that the four are needed: **a software decoder reads the same symbol rendered with no margin at all**, measured, so a passing round trip is not evidence for them. The four rests on the specification and on a camera having to separate the symbol from everything printed around it. The check asserts the zero-margin decode explicitly, so the limit is in the tier rather than only in this paragraph.

**THE ENDPOINT IS GONE, AND #352 IS WHERE IT WENT.** Three paragraphs stood here describing `GET /api/tool-items/[toolItemId]/qr` — that it read the record to refuse an id with no row, that its two operations per symbol are what barred a sheet from it, and that it was gated on `getActiveUser()` and cached for a year. **What killed it is that the caller it was built for turned out not to want it.** #351 named the tool item's own page as the single-label consumer, and when that page came to render one it had already read the session and that very record — so the endpoint's two operations were the same two facts asked a second time, and the read that justified one of them (refusing a missing id) had already been performed by the page's own not-found return. With the sheet importing the builder and the page importing the builder, nothing called the address. This axis deletes those rather than keeping them: #340's own commit deleted `getToolItemByRecordId` for exactly this and kept `getToolItemsByRecordIds` because it had a caller.

**WHAT DIED WITH IT AND WHAT SURVIVED, because most of #351 was never about the route.** Gone: the endpoint, its `authz-structure.mjs` exemption (a stale one fails, so the deletion was forced to be complete), `QR_ROUTE` and `toolItemQRPath`, and `offline/tool-routes.mjs`' six assertions about that address — replaced by one asserting the axis serves no Route Handler at all, since an address deleted for having no caller is one a later pass could reintroduce by habit. Surviving untouched: the version, the error-correction level, the quiet zone, the library choice, the independent decode, and every figure in the table above — **all of it belongs to `lib/toolLabelQR.js`, which now has two real callers instead of one endpoint and one prediction.** The one piece of reasoning worth carrying forward from the exemption is a general rule rather than a fact about a route, and `authorization.md` keeps it: one gate and no field to select is what makes an exemption the whole coverage.

**AND THE ARGUMENT THAT BARRED A SHEET FROM THE ENDPOINT DID NOT DEPEND ON THE ENDPOINT.** Two operations per symbol against a base limited to five requests a second is why a hundred labels could not be fetched one at a time — and what that really established is that a screen showing many symbols reads its tool items in ONE batched query and builds the symbols itself, which is `getToolItemsByToolItemIds` and is #193's standing ban on per-record reads. That shape stands whether or not an address exists to be barred from.

**A `Retired` TOOL ITEM STILL GETS A SYMBOL**, which the endpoint's paragraph used to say and is now the page's: its label may be on the object, and a reprint is the same act whatever the status says.

**A SYMBOL LOOKED AT LOCALLY IS NOT THE ONE A LABEL CARRIES.** The origin comes off the request, so `HTTP://LOCALHOST:3000/T/HYE-TL-260909-004` is 41 characters and encodes as **version 3, 29 modules, 37 on a side** — measured, and asserted in the check so the two figures cannot be confused. That is the honest consequence of a label encoding where it was printed from, and it is also the second reason a label may not be printed from a preview host: a Vercel preview domain is longer still. **#411 TOOK THAT TELL AWAY AND `What the label drops, and what that bought` IS WHERE THE LOSS IS RECORDED** — the local address is 34 characters now and encodes at the same version as the printed one.

**THE LIBRARY IS `qrcode`, AND THE THING IT WAS CHOSEN FOR IS THE READ-BACK.** `create()` reports the version, the module count and the segment modes, so the mode is a measurement rather than an instruction — which is what turned #348's alphanumeric claim into evidence. `qrcode-generator` was the other candidate, zero-dependency and agreeing on every version, and it lost because its mode is an argument the caller passes: there is nothing left to measure. The cost is recorded rather than glossed: `require("qrcode")` pulls `dijkstrajs` and `pngjs` into the server graph (59 modules) for renderers this app never calls, and `yargs` sits in the install tree reachable only from the package's CLI. Its own SVG renderer is used rather than a hand-rolled one — 1210 bytes against 2346 for a path we would write ourselves, with the margin and the absent `width`/`height` already right, and rendering from `create`'s matrix would be a second implementation of one rule. The two encodes per call that costs (114us and 104us, measured) are noise against an Airtable round trip. **#467 reversed this, on a print**: the library draws each run of dark modules as a stroke, and Chrome and Edge print a stroke under a point as a hairline, so the symbol is drawn from `create`'s matrix in fills — one encode per call, 2,314 bytes — and `A label is a page of its own` has the measurement.

**THE DECODE IS A DIFFERENT LIBRARY, AND THAT IS THE POINT.** `jsqr` is a devDependency for the offline tier alone, the way `acorn` is: an encoder decoding its own output proves only that it is self-consistent. jsQR reads the format information, corrects with its own implementation, and reports version 2 and an `alphanumeric` chunk — its answers are compared against the encoder's rather than assumed to match, and six minted ids round-trip. **What that does not cover is the rendering**, because the check decodes the MATRIX and not the served SVG bytes; the browser measurement in #351's pull request is what closes it, by painting the real response and decoding the pixels. **Since #467 the check covers it too**: it fills the served SVG's own path into a raster and decodes that, beside the library's stroked rows drawn as a hairline, which do not decode.

### The sheet the symbol is printed on (#353)

`/tool-items/labels` takes printed ids in its address and draws a sheet of adhesive stock at its true size. `lib/toolLabelSheet.js` holds the stock, the arithmetic and the words; `offline/tool-label-sheet.mjs` holds the figures by value. **#467 replaced the sheet with a page per label**, and the stock, the grid, the start position and `size: letter` went with it; the file is `lib/toolLabelPage.js` and the check `offline/tool-label-page.mjs` since, and `A label is a page of its own` has what replaced each. The ids in the address, the include, the cap and the screen-only class were still this section's until **#457 took all but the cap with the screen** — `The labels as a dialog` has what replaced them.

**THE STOCK IS NOT CHOSEN, AND THAT IS THE CONSTRAINT THE WHOLE FILE IS SHAPED BY.** Nobody has confirmed which printer the site has, so no stock has been bought. The layout was therefore built against `Avery 5160` — 30 labels of 25.4 x 66.675 mm on US Letter, the sheet an office printer takes — with every figure in `LABEL_STOCK` and none of them written twice. **#412 replaced that stock with one sized from the symbol and nothing about this arrangement changed, which is the test below passing rather than a claim about it.** **The test that matters is whether the real stock costs a layout edit, and it does not: one object changes.** The single exception is `@page size`, which the language will not let a custom property reach, so `labels.css` carries the keyword and the check compares the two — the arrangement `LABEL_REWRITE` already has with `next.config.mjs` (#348).

**THE MODULE SIZE IS DERIVED FROM THE STOCK AND THE VERSION HEADROOM, NOT CHOSEN.** #351 handed over a count rather than a millimeter, and the arithmetic that turns one into the other is: the side of the label only the symbol spans — its height while the code sat beside the symbol, its width since #431 — less two safe insets, divided by the widest symbol the printed size must absorb, rounded down to a hundredth. That was **0.57 mm per module** on Avery 5160, which put today's version-2 symbol at 18.81 mm and the version-4 symbol two steps above it at 23.37 mm inside a 23.4 mm budget. Choosing a millimeter figure instead would have meant re-deriving it by hand for the real stock, which is the edit this file exists to avoid. **#412 kept the derivation pointing the same way and moved the label under it: 0.4 mm per module, today's symbol at 13.2 mm and the version-3 symbol one step above it at 14.8 mm inside a 14.8 mm budget.** **#453 took that step: today's symbol is the widest, 13.2 mm inside a 13.2 mm budget.**

**TWO VERSIONS OF HEADROOM, AND THE REASON IS #351's MEASUREMENT RATHER THAN CAUTION.** The printed address used exactly version 2's capacity — 38 alphanumeric characters of 38 — so a four-digit daily sequence was already version 3, and the production host is not settled either: the app is on a Vercel domain today and every character of a longer host counts against the same budget. Sizing per module for the largest of the three means a longer address prints a bigger symbol rather than a denser one, which is the only version of that trade where the label still scans.

**#411 REMOVED ONE OF THOSE TWO REASONS AND #412 SPENT IT: `VERSION_HEADROOM` IS 1.** The sequence is no longer a claimant — four digits and five both stay at version 2, measured — and the host is the one that remains, worth a single step. **What makes zero the wrong answer rather than the smallest one** is that the module is at its floor now, so a version step cannot be absorbed by a thinner module the way #353 absorbed one; it has to be a bigger label, and with none at all `symbolBox` reports the symbol as not fitting and the screen draws no label for that tool item. One step costs 1.6 mm on the dimension that decides whether the sticker goes on a wrench. **#453 took the other claimant's step too, and `VERSION_HEADROOM` is 0** — `The label for today's symbol` has why the reason this paragraph gives stopped holding.

**THE MODULE SITS ON A FLOOR THAT IS A CITATION AND NOT A MEASUREMENT.** `MIN_MODULE_MM` is 0.4 mm, the practical minimum published for a phone camera resolving a printed module; **nothing in this repository measured it and nothing in this repository can.** What would settle it is printing a sheet on real stock and scanning it with a real phone, and neither has happened. **#353 sat 0.17 mm above that bound and #412 sits ON it**, which is the same dependence one step sharper: the label was solved backwards from the floor, so there is no arithmetic left between the two. **The day somebody prints a sheet and scans it, this number reopens** — and it now takes the label's own dimensions with it, where before it would only have moved the module. **A sheet printed from a local server is that sheet since #453**, its address being version 2 at the production geometry, so a Vercel domain drawing none closes nothing. The check holds the derived module above the floor, which turns "this stock is too small for a scannable symbol" into a failing check rather than a discovery on paper. **#467 was that print, on a label printer rather than a sheet, and the floor is a measurement: 0.29 mm.**

**THE READABLE CODE HAS A FLOOR AND NO CEILING, WHICH IS WHERE THIS ISSUE'S AUTHORITY STOPS.** That the code must be legible is functional — it is the only path from a scratched or painted-over symbol back to the record — so the code's floor is set here — `MIN_ID_FONT_PT` since #431, `MIN_ID_FONT_MM` before it — and the check requires the code to fit under the widest symbol at that size. **#353 SET IT AND RECORDED NOTHING ABOUT WHERE IT CAME FROM, WHICH #412 REPAIRED ALONG WITH THE VALUE.** The paragraph above gives the module's floor a source and a condition for reopening; this one got a number, 2.5 mm, and neither — so a figure that is exactly as quoted and exactly as unmeasured read as though somebody had established it. #412 made it 2.0 mm, cited as the height below which an ordinary office printer stops holding a character's strokes apart. **#431 made it 6 pt**, the size print convention holds as the smallest legible, because that convention states a font size and the sentence #412 wrote read as a height of ink — `The code under the symbol` has why. It reopens on the same event the module's floor does. **The difference between #353's value and #412's is the reading distance**: #353 set both for a label read at arm's length, and this label is read in the hand, off a tool somebody is holding. Everything above the floor is the design's: how much larger, where it sits, what it is set in. It renders AT the floor, and since #431 that is the size the design kept when it chose the face rather than the constraint with no design applied. **#467 was the event it reopens on as well: 5 pt, measured.**

**THE FIT IS THE ONE PHYSICAL CLAIM AN OFFLINE CHECK CAN HOLD.** Whether a label overlaps its neighbor, whether ink lands in the quiet zone, whether a browser scaled the page — none of that is visible to a tier that never renders. What is arithmetic is whether the pieces fit — since #431, the widest symbol against the label's usable width, and the widest symbol plus a gap plus the code's one em against its usable height, with the code at its floor also clearing the width under the symbol. **If they do not fit, no layout can stop them overlapping**, and ink inside the quiet zone is exactly what #351 asked this issue not to produce. So `labelBudget` returns the parts and the check adds them up.

**AND THE STOCK'S OWN GEOMETRY IS ADDED BACK UP RATHER THAN TRUSTED.** The published numbers are a margin, a pitch and a count, and they only reach 215.9 x 279.4 mm if every one was transcribed correctly — so the check requires the left margin plus the pitches plus the last label's width to land the same margin on the right. **The first version of that assertion rounded to hundredths and failed on its own arithmetic**: 3/16 of an inch is 4.7625 mm and 1/8 is 3.175, and two decimals destroy both. It rounds to four now.

**THE FIGURES ARE ASSERTED BY VALUE, WHICH IS #351's LESSON APPLIED BEFORE IT COULD BE REPEATED.** That issue expressed every quiet-zone assertion in terms of `QR_QUIET_ZONE_MODULES` and measured the consequence: changing 4 to 2 moved the constant, the derived side, the SVG's margin and the builder's report together, and all 47 assertions passed. Every figure on this screen is reachable from `LABEL_STOCK`, so the same trap is larger here — a check written against it would pass for any stock, including one too small to print. So the literals are typed out, and changing stock changes them in the same commit.

**THE SELECTION CROSSES A NAVIGATION, WHICH IS WHY THE IDS ARE IN THE ADDRESS.** A registration's minted ids live in the action's return value and nowhere else — a reload loses them — so the link that leaves the account is the only way they reach another screen. **Since #449 they reach it through the tool's page**, which a registration lands on with them selected; the link that left the account went with the account. They are the PRINTED ids rather than record ids, because that is what the account holds, what a person reads off a sticker, and what one batched query can look up. `id` is repeatable and is **a fifth kind of URL parameter** in `offline/url-parameters.mjs`' inventory: not a filter (there is no list without it), not a navigation onto a record, not a slice, and not an account. **It is clear of #321 on that last distinction** — it says what the next act is for, so a copied link is a good request to print those labels again rather than somebody else's confirmation. **#443 gave that kind a second member rather than adding a seventh** — a tool's list carries its selection under this name — and `Which tool items a label run prints` below says why. **#457 took the navigation away**: the labels are a dialog over the page holding the selection, which hands the ids to the dialog's read, so the first member went with its screen.

**AND THE SCREEN SELECTS AMONG THEM RATHER THAN PRINTING WHAT IT WAS HANDED.** The address carries candidates and a per-label include decides. That is the app's first row selection: every checkbox on every other list in this app was a FILTER — `over` on the deliveries list, `mine` on the two document lists — and none of them selected a row, **until #443 put the second on a tool's own list.** Which also settles the cap: `MAX_LABELS_PER_REQUEST` is `MAX_TOOL_ITEMS_PER_REGISTRATION` rather than a number of its own, because a cap below it would silently drop the tail of the largest registration anybody can make. **#457 took the include**: the dialog prints what it is handed, and the tool's list is the selection.

**THE START POSITION IS NOT AN EDGE CASE, AND THE ARITHMETIC IS WHY IT EXISTS.** A registration is usually a handful of tools, so a run of six on a sheet of a hundred and forty-three leaves a hundred and thirty-seven positions — and printing always from the top would throw away most of a sheet every time somebody registers anything. The control is a POSITION rather than a count of labels to skip, because a person looks at a part-used sheet and counts across to the first one still attached. It clamps rather than refusing, which is `pageOfToolItems`' shape (#339) for the same reason: the value comes from a control somebody types into.

**THE HOST IS NAMED ON THE SCREEN, AND THIS IS WHERE A RULE BECAME A RISK.** #340 recorded that no label may be printed before the app has the host it will keep, and until this screen existed that was a rule with nothing to attach to. A symbol encodes the host it was printed from, so a sheet printed on a preview domain is thirty stickers pointing at somewhere that stops resolving — and **the app is on a Vercel domain today, so it is the live case rather than a hypothetical.** The screen states the host and says so, on screen and never on the label. It does not refuse: nothing in the app knows which host is the permanent one, and a guard that guessed would block every local check. **Since #453 the geometry refuses a host long enough to step the version**, which guesses nothing and blocks no local check — `The label for today's symbol` has how. **#454 took the host and the warning off the screen**, so the rule is back to having nothing on it to attach to — `The host off the screen` has why.

**THE PRINT PATH IS THE APP'S FIRST, MEASURED BEFORE IT WAS WRITTEN**: no `@page`, no `@media print` and no physical unit anywhere under `app/` or `lib/`. Three rules carry it. `size: letter` makes the page box match the paper, so the browser's default scale is already correct and there is nothing to fit. `margin: 0` is load-bearing rather than tidy — a default page margin of about half an inch would push every label off its die-cut by more than a label's height, and the stock's own top and left margins are what position the first one. And `@media print` hides the picker, the position control and every sentence, so the paper carries labels and nothing else. **What no CSS can do is refuse a reader who prints at `Fit to page`**, which is stated in the stylesheet's own header where somebody debugging a wrong size will meet it. **Since #467 the page box is the label's own, 11 x 12 mm, and each label is a page.** **Since #457 it is a named page the root takes only while the dialog is open**, and the print keeps the pages and hides the rest.

**A SHEET IS ABSOLUTE PLACEMENT AND NOT A GRID.** The offsets belong to the die-cut, and a grid would distribute the gaps that the cut has already fixed. Each label is positioned from the page corner by `cellPosition`, which is the only place the pitches are read. **`cellPosition` went with the sheet (#467).**

**AND THE FIRST REAL PRINT FOUND WHAT NONE OF THAT COULD.** The print rule hid `.label-controls`, which is the sheet component's picker — and the `<h1>` is rendered by the PAGE, so it printed: 24 pixels of heading took page one, and every sheet moved down by a page. The stylesheet's own comment claimed to hide the heading and the navigation, which made it a false comment sitting directly above the rule that did not. **The lesson is the arithmetic rather than the oversight**: a sheet is exactly one page tall against a page box with no margin, so the set of things hidden at print has to be exhaustive rather than nearly so, and "nearly" is invisible until paper comes out. The class is named for the property now — everything that is not a label is inside one `label-screen-only` wrapper on both files — and `offline/tool-label-page.mjs` walks the JSX of both, requiring every heading, sentence, link and control to have that class above it. That check is structural because it has to be: nothing in this tier renders, and the browser pane cannot reach a print preview. **#457 turned the rule round and the class went**: the print keeps the label pages and hides everything else — the page behind the dialog, its head, its column and its backdrop — so nothing is off the paper by being listed, and what can still reach it is something set beside a label in the pane, which the check reads the pane's tree for.

**WHAT NOBODY HAS DONE, AND IT IS THE HALF THAT DECIDES WHETHER THIS WORKS.** No sheet has been printed on paper, no ruler has been put to one, and no phone has scanned a printed label. What was checked is the browser's own layout measured back in millimeters and its print preview; #353's pull request carries both and says what they are. **The gap is not a disclaimer but the condition on two numbers above** — the module size and its floor — and closing it is a physical act this repository cannot perform. **#467 is where a label was printed and a phone read it**, on the label printer, at the geometry `A label is a page of its own` records.

### The screens the labels come from are a desk's

**`/tools`, the registration dialog over it (`/tools/new` until #456) and `/tools/[toolRecordId]` are drawn for a desktop and `/tool-items/[toolItemId]` for a phone**, and each brief now says so beside its reader. The tool item's page is the one tools screen a phone shows, and the sign-in screen with it, since a scan can arrive there signed out; the list, the registration dialog, the tool's page and the labels' dialog are used at a desk and do not support a phone's width.

The split follows what the person is doing rather than where they are. A site has laptops and monitors too. Entering a tool's name and a quantity is typing, comparing counts across the catalog is scanning a table, and **the label printing that Phase 2 adds happens at whatever machine the printer is attached to — so that screen is a desk's as well, and this sentence is here so that issue does not decide it again.** The one screen a scan arrives at is the tool item's, and that is the phone's.

### What the label drops, and what that bought (#411)

The printed address is `HTTPS://APP.HYEUSA.COM/T/260909-004` — `HTTPS://HYEUSA.COM/T/260909-004` until #467 — and the sticker prints `260909-004`. `Tool Items."Tool Item ID"` is unchanged — `HYE-TL-260909-004`, on every screen and every list — and `lib/toolRoutes.js` holds both halves of the conversion.

**SEVEN CHARACTERS THAT SEPARATED NOTHING, AND THAT IS THE WHOLE ARGUMENT.** `HYE-TL-` opens every tool item on this base, so it confirmed nothing when somebody held a sticker against this app's screen and it bought nothing in the symbol. What it cost was measurable in two places at once: the encoded address and the width of the readable code, which are the two physical dimensions of a label.

**THE VERSION DID NOT MOVE AND THE HEADROOM DID, WHICH IS THE FIGURE THIS ISSUE IS FOR.** Measured at M against `https://hyeusa.com`: 31 characters, version 2, 25 modules of symbol, 33 on a side — identical geometry to the 38-character address. What changed is that version 2 holds 38 alphanumeric characters and the address now leaves **seven spare** where it left none. That buys a daily sequence of five digits (`260909-10000` is 33 characters and still version 2) or a host of up to seventeen characters, or a share of each; 39 is where the next version starts, and the check encodes exactly that boundary rather than asserting the headroom as a number on its own. **Since #453 the next version is a label the labels' dialog refuses**, so the seven decide whether a label prints. **At `app.hyeusa.com` (#467) the address is 35 characters and three are spare**, which a six-digit daily sequence fills.

**THE PRINTED CODE AND THE PATH SEGMENT ARE ONE STRING, AND THE FALLBACK IS WHY.** The readable code exists for a symbol somebody has scratched or painted over, and what that person does with it is type it into the address — so printing one form while routing another breaks the path at the only point it is ever used. That also settles what the sticker may not carry: anything the address does not.

**THE REATTACHMENT IS NOT PART OF `canonicalToolItemId`, AND FOLDING IT IN WOULD HAVE SHIPPED A DEFECT.** That function's assertion is an IDENTITY — a minted id equals its own canonical form, held against `formatSequentialId` — which a function that also prepends a token cannot make. And it has a second caller: `/tool-items/labels` canonicalizes the ids named in its own address, and those are whole `Tool Item ID`s straight from a registration's account when this was written and from a tool's page since #449, so a folded-in token would have prefixed them twice. `labelCodeFor` and `toolItemIdFromLabelCode` are separate and compose with it; the second canonicalizes as it reattaches, so the destination still has nothing to redirect and **a scan is still one hop**. **Since #457 the second caller is the labels' read**, on the ids a tool's page hands it.

**THE OLD FORM IS REFUSED, AND WHAT MADE THAT CHEAP IS THAT NOTHING CARRIES IT.** No label has been printed; nothing under `app/` or `lib/` links to `/t/`; every symbol is built as a page renders, so even a camera pointed at this app's own screen picks up the current form. There is therefore no artifact anywhere holding the old segment, and a branch admitting it would have no caller — #340's ground for refusing a comparison it could not reach, and #352's for deleting an endpoint nothing called. `/t/HYE-TL-260909-004` gets the token put in front of it like any other string, resolves to no tool item, and meets `Tool item not found`. **What was not checked is the server's access log**, which this repository cannot reach; what was checked is that no artifact exists to have produced such a request.

**THE ONE THING THIS ISSUE TOOK AWAY IS A SAFEGUARD, AND IT IS RECORDED AS A LOSS.** #351 measured a dev origin at version 3 against production's version 2, and asserted it, precisely so a figure read in a browser could not be mistaken for the printed one. At 34 characters `HTTP://LOCALHOST:3000/T/260909-004` is version 2 with the same 25 modules, so **the geometry no longer tells a local symbol from a real one** and only the payload does. A Vercel preview domain is 45 characters and still steps a version, so the tell survives for the case that needed it least. What stands between a person and a sheet of stickers pointing at a host that will stop resolving is now the sentence `/tool-items/labels` prints above the sheet, and nothing else — which is why that sentence is not decoration and may not be moved below the fold. **#453 made the surviving half a refusal**: a preview domain's sheet is not drawn at all, so the sentence stands alone only for a host short enough to stay at version 2. **#454 took the sentence off**, with the host above it, so for that host what stands there is #340's rule and nothing on the screen.
