**AND WHAT #412 IS HANDED IS WIDTH, WHICH IS NOT WHAT THIS ISSUE'S OWN BODY PREDICTED.** The body said the readable code was wide enough to set the label's width; the arithmetic says the HEIGHT binds and always did. On Avery 5160 the widest symbol the module size absorbs is 23.37 mm against 23.4 mm of usable height — 0.03 mm — while the code at its floor claims 15.0 mm of the 39.81 mm beside it, leaving **24.81 mm of width unclaimed** where seventeen characters left 14.31. So every `fits` verdict is unchanged by this issue, measured at 33, 37, 41 and 45 modules a side, and what moved is the room. `ID_CHARACTERS` is 10 and is the one figure #412 needs before it can derive anything.

### The label sized from the symbol (#412)

The label is **30.3 x 16.8 mm**, from 66.675 x 25.4; the module is **0.4 mm** from 0.57, the readable code **2.0 mm** from 2.5, the version headroom **1** from 2, and a Letter sheet holds **78** where it held 30. `LABEL_STOCK` still holds every figure and `labels.css` still carries the one keyword a custom property cannot reach. **These are #412's figures, and #431 moved three of them** — 16.8 x 20.42 mm with the code under the symbol, 110 a sheet, the code's floor 6 pt — **and #453 the headroom, the label and the sheet again**: 15.2 x 17.32 mm, 143, a headroom of 0. See `The code under the symbol` and `The label for today's symbol`. **#467 replaced the sheet with a page per label — 11 x 12 mm, 0.29 mm a module, the code at 5 pt — and `A label is a page of its own` has it.**

**THE DIRECTION OF THE DERIVATION DID NOT CHANGE, WHICH IS WHY THIS COST ONE OBJECT.** `moduleSizeMm` still reads the label's height and reports a module; what moved is the label under it, chosen so the answer lands on the floor instead of wherever a product put it. `labelSizeMm` is the same arithmetic solved the other way — floors in, label out — and the two are a second path to one pair of numbers: the check requires `LABEL_STOCK` to satisfy the derivation, and on this label it EQUALS it. Inverting the function instead would have been the larger change and would have left the real-stock case with nothing to check against. **#431 turned the side it reads from the height to the width**, which is the side only the symbol spans once the code is under it; the equality the check requires stood.

**THE GRID IS DERIVED NOW, AND A COUNT THAT IS MERELY CORRECT IS WHAT THAT GUARDS AGAINST.** #353 typed `columns`, `rows` and both pitches into `LABEL_STOCK` because they were a product's published geometry; nothing here is transcribed, so they are computed from the page, the margin, the label and the gap. The check asserts MAXIMALITY rather than a sum — every label keeps the far margin and one more of either would not — and it reads the call off the AST, because **a count pinned to the right number passes every figure in the file**. Measured: replacing the derivation with a literal `6` left all 5,384 checks green and a literal `3` failed eight, so the values caught a wrong count and nothing caught one that had stopped tracking the stock.

**WHAT DECIDES WHETHER THE STICKER GOES ON IS THE NARROW SIDE, AND BOTH ARRANGEMENTS GIVE THE SAME ONE.** A label wraps along a handle, so the narrow side has to clear the handle's width while the long side runs down a tool far longer than either. Stacking the code under the symbol was computed rather than dismissed: **16.8 x 23.1 mm against 16.8 x 30.3, and 100 labels a sheet against 78.** The narrow side is 16.8 either way, because the symbol is wider than the code at its floor and the symbol is what sets it. So the arrangement does not answer this issue's question, and it stays side by side — where the tool's name, which has no length limit, sits in height the symbol has already paid for rather than deciding the label's own. The figures are in `docs/briefs/tool-items-labels.md` so a design asking for the other one arrives with them. **THE REASON THIS GAVE WAS THE NAME, AND #431 IS WHERE IT WENT**: the design dropped the tool's name from the label, which left the side-by-side arrangement carrying nothing a stacked one could not, and the code went under the symbol. **#453 moved the narrow side itself, to 15.2 mm, by taking the headroom rather than by rearranging anything.**

**IT DOES NOT FIT THE NARROW END OF THE RANGE THE ISSUE NAMES, AND THAT IS STATED RATHER THAN ROUNDED PAST.** A wrench handle is roughly 15 to 20 mm and the narrow side is 16.8. **At 15 mm it does not fit, and nothing available here makes it fit**: zero version headroom would reach 15.2 mm and still miss, and below that the three things left are all fixed from outside this repository. The symbol's eight modules of quiet zone are the specification's and #351 refused to spend them on the evidence that a software decoder needs none. The two 1 mm safe insets are the allowance for a die-cut that is not perfectly placed and a sheet that does not feed perfectly straight. The 0.4 mm module is a citation this issue may not measure its way past — which is the same sentence as the reopening condition, read from the other end. **So a tool with a handle under about 17 mm has no label yet.** **#453 spent that headroom: the narrow side is 15.2 mm, so a label now misses a handle under about 15.2 mm — and 15 mm still misses, by exactly this paragraph's arithmetic.**

**AND THE LABEL HAS NO SLACK IN EITHER DIRECTION, WHICH IS BOTH THE POINT AND THE ONE HAZARD WORTH RECORDING.** The widest symbol is 14.8 mm against 14.8 mm of budget across and down alike, so `symbolBox`'s two comparisons are equalities. They hold — every figure is a sum of the same few constants and the floats land on the same value, checked rather than assumed — but a constant moved by a hundredth would put one of them on the wrong side of a rounding step with nothing on screen to show it. **Nothing has been observed doing that**, and what would catch it is the pair of budgets and the three `fits` verdicts, pinned by value. **Stacked (#431), across is still an equality and down carries 0.0033 mm**, which is the label's height rounded up to a hundredth from what six points comes to. **#453 kept both at 13.2 mm: an equality across and 0.0033 mm down.**

**MEASURED IN A BROWSER, WHICH IS WHERE #353's OWN DEFECT WAS FOUND.** At 1440 and at 375 the label cell renders 30.3 x 16.8 mm, the symbol 13.2 mm across a 33-module `viewBox` — **0.4 mm a module** — and the code at 2.0 mm. The tool item page draws the same 13.2 mm, so #352's equality survives the resize. The code itself renders **10.67 mm**, a character-width ratio of 0.534 against the 0.6 the budget assumes, so the budget still over-estimates in the safe direction; **that citation was two issues stale** — it quoted 23.46 mm for a 17-character id at a 2.5 mm floor, neither of which the label has carried since #411 — and it is #412's measurement now. **That 0.534 was Arial**, the body's face, because nothing on the tools screens set one; `The code under the symbol` records it.

**WHAT THE BROWSER COULD NOT SHOW IS A VERSION STEP, AND #411 IS WHY.** #353 caught its sizing defect by looking at a localhost symbol, which was version 3 against production's version 2; the shortened address put the dev origin at version 2 as well, so this branch has no way to render a larger symbol without forging a host. What holds the per-label sizing instead is the check — 33, 37 and 41 modules at 13.2, 14.8 and 16.4 mm, the module width identical at each — and section 7's AST assertion that the page passes each symbol's own side count rather than the constant. **#431 forged one**, for the measurement and put back after it, and the version step was drawn — see that section.

### The code under the symbol (#431)

The label is **16.8 x 20.42 mm** with the code under the symbol, from 30.3 x 16.8 with it beside, and a Letter sheet holds **110** where it held 78. The module is **0.4 mm** and the version headroom **1**, both unmoved. The code's floor is **6 pt** where it was 2.0 mm, and the code prints in **Inconsolata**, whose advance — half its size per character — is `CHARACTER_WIDTH_RATIO` in place of the 0.6 over-estimate. **These are #431's figures, and #453 moved the label, the sheet, the headroom and the gap** — `The label for today's symbol` below — **and #467 every one of them but the face and its advance**, in `A label is a page of its own`.

**THE ARRANGEMENT WAS KEPT FOR THE NAME, AND THE NAME IS GONE.** #412 computed the stacked label and kept the side-by-side one because the tool's name, which has no length limit, sat in height the symbol had already paid for. The design has since dropped the name from the label. **The drop happened here in the code, not before:** until this issue `LabelSheet.js` still printed `label.toolName` inside the cell and `offline/tool-label-sheet.mjs` asserted two things printed on a label — only the design, which does not read this repository, had let it go. So the label carries the symbol and the code and nothing else. The picker above the labels still names each tool item by its tool: it is a screen naming a record, and a person choosing which labels to print chooses by the name. The page reads `Tools` for the picker exactly as it did. **#457 took the picker and that read**: the dialog names the opening page's tool under its title.

**THE NARROW SIDE DID NOT MOVE, AND THE LONG SIDE IS WHAT STACKING BOUGHT.** The symbol is wider than the code at its floor, so the narrow side is the symbol's alone in both arrangements — 16.8 mm, the height side by side and the width stacked. The long side went from the symbol, a gap and ten characters (30.3) to the symbol, a gap and one em of code (20.42). #412's stacked figure was 16.8 x 23.1 and 100 a sheet: it carried the name as a second line, and the two lines took 4.8 mm of it.

**`moduleSizeMm` READS THE WIDTH, AND NO FIGURE CAN SAY SO.** It reads the side only the symbol spans, which was the height side by side and is the width stacked. Its output cannot show which: the width leaves the widest symbol 14.8 mm and the height, after the gap and the code, 14.8033 — and both floor to the same 0.4 mm module. So the check reads the `LABEL_STOCK` member `moduleSizeMm` touches off the AST, and a mutation pointing it at the height's room passes every value in the file and fails that one assertion. `symbolBox` has the same shape one step along: with its two rooms that close, dropping either comparison changes no verdict, so the check reads both clauses off the source too. `labelSizeMm` is still `moduleSizeMm` solved the other way round, and the check still requires the stock to equal it.

**ONE LINE OF CODE IS ONE EM, AND THE COMPONENT HAS TO SAY SO.** The label's height counts the code at its size, which is true only if the code's line box is its size. Left to inherit, the line box is the page's — Tailwind's preflight sets `line-height: 1.5` on `html` — and the code would claim 3.2 mm of a 2.1167 mm slot, running into the bottom inset whenever the widest symbol is drawn. So `.label-id` sets its `lineHeight` from the same name as its size, and the check holds the two together. **Measured that one em is enough:** Inconsolata's digits and hyphen carry ink 0.641 em above the baseline and 0.016 below, and in the 2.1167 mm line box that leaves 0.23 mm above the ink and 0.50 below it. A leading of 1.2 would have made the label 20.84 mm and the sheet the same 110. **#467 corrected both figures and dropped the em**: the face's outlines give 0.631 and 0.011, those two were a canvas's raster bounds, and the code's height is its ink.

**THE CODE'S FLOOR IS IN POINTS BECAUSE ITS SOURCE IS.** It was `MIN_ID_FONT_MM = 2.0`, which is 5.67 pt — just under the 6 pt print convention holds as the smallest legible, and not by intent: every other figure on the label is in millimeters and this one followed. #412 recorded its source as the height below which an office printer stops holding a character's strokes apart, which reads as the height of the INK, while the convention it rested on speaks of a font size. That reading is what raised the question here: at 2.0 mm of font size Inconsolata's digits stand about 1.3 mm tall, so a floor read as an ink height would have been a different number. **It is a font size**, and moving the unit to points is what makes the value and its source say the same thing. The label grows 0.117 mm for it — the height comes to 20.4167 mm and rounds up to 20.42 — and the count stays 110. **Reading it as an ink height was weighed and refused**: 2.0 mm of ink is over 3 mm of font size, which puts ten characters at more than 15 mm, wider than the symbol — and the width would stop being the symbol's, which is the premise `moduleSizeMm` reading the width rests on.

**THE HEIGHT IS ROUNDED UP, AND IT IS THE ONE ROUNDING HERE THAT MOVES A FIGURE.** Six points is 2.1167 mm and lands on no hundredth, so `labelSizeMm` rounds the die-cut UP to a hundredth — `moduleSizeMm`'s floor turned round, for the same reason — and `labelBudget` hands back the code's two needs unrounded, since a need rounded to any step can come out smaller than it is. That leaves the code 0.0033 mm.

**THE RATIO IS A MEASUREMENT, AND #412's 0.534 WAS ARIAL.** 0.5 was read three ways and all three agree: out of the WOFF2 file `next/font` serves (`unitsPerEm` 1000, every digit and the hyphen at 500, `isFixedPitch` set), from the browser's own text measurement (500 at 1000 px for each of the eleven), and as the rendered width of `260909-004` at the floor, 10.5833 mm — ten characters at half of 2.1167 exactly. **The 0.534 #412 cited was not a face anybody chose.** No class on the tools screens set a type, so the code inherited `globals.css`'s `body { font-family: Arial, … }`, and Arial measures 0.5338 over the same ten characters. A figure read in that state stood in the budget as though it described the label, and the empty container #336 left is what kept that out of sight: nothing anywhere said what the code was set in, so nothing could say it was not the face the budget assumed.

**THE FACE ENTERS THE CODE HERE, AND ONLY FOR THE CODE.** The ratio is one face's advance, so the code has to print in that face or the budget describes letters that are not on the paper. The labels page loads Inconsolata through `next/font` into `--font-label-code`, defines it on the element above the labels, and `.label-id` is the one rule set in it. **Since #457 the dialog loads it and defines it on the pane's list.** **It does not reach the root layout or the tools layout**: those are where a design is applied to screens, and #336 left the second empty for that — filling it for one measurement would be deciding the axis's type in passing. **Where the dependency is written, and why there:** the ratio's docstring and `LABEL_CODE_TYPEFACE` hold the measurement beside the face it was taken in; the page's loader and the `.label-id` rule each say what changing them reopens; `offline/tool-label-page.mjs` holds the chain from the constant to the paper and pins the face and the width by value, so a face changed at either end fails a check; and `docs/briefs/tool-items-labels.md` carries it to the design, which does not read this repository. **Not in CLAUDE.md**, by the audience test: `.label-id` declares its own face, so no face chosen for the rest of the app reaches the code by inheritance, and the only way to change it is to edit this route — the dialog since #457 — whose editor this file and that check already reach.

**THE RATIO NO LONGER DECIDES A DIMENSION.** Beside the symbol, ten characters were a term in the label's long side; under it they share the symbol's width, 10.58 mm of 14.8. That is the one room on the label: thirteen characters fit at the floor, a five-digit daily sequence included, and a face up to 0.69 of its size per character fits without the label changing. The fallback `next/font` declares while the face loads — Arial at a `size-adjust` of 112.16%, which comes to 0.6 — is inside that, and the page's header records it as the unobserved case of a print pressed before the face arrived. **#457 made the press wait for the face**, since the dialog first asks for it when it opens rather than on arrival. **#453 narrowed that room with the width**: 10.58 of 13.2 mm, twelve characters — a five-digit sequence exactly — and a face up to 0.62, which the fallback's 0.6 still clears. **#467 narrowed it again**: 8.82 of 9.57 mm, ten characters, and a face up to 0.54, which the fallback no longer clears — its ten would be 10.58 mm, 0.51 past the symbol either side and 0.21 inside the label's edges.

**A SHEET HOLDS MORE THAN ONE REQUEST PRINTS NOW.** 110 against a cap of 100, which turned an assertion round: it said a run always spans sheets. The largest request fits one sheet from the top and spills onto a second only from a start position past eleven, so both sides of that edge are pinned and the second sheet stays a state the screen reaches. The same change put `readStartPosition("99")` inside the sheet, where it had stood for "past the end" only while a sheet held fewer. **143 since #453, so the edge is a start position past forty-four.** **#467 took the sheet and the start position**, so a request of a hundred is a hundred pages.

**WHAT THE CHECK HOLDS, AND WHAT ONLY THE SOURCE COULD.** Which side the module is read from, which way the two pieces stack, what the code's line box is and what face it prints in are all facts no sum can see — a label drawn with the code on top adds up exactly as this one does. So each is read off the AST or the stylesheet beside a planted counterexample. **Twenty-one mutations were run and all twenty-one fail the file**; three of them change no figure at all — `moduleSizeMm` reading the height's room, and `symbolBox` losing either clause — and fail only on the source.

**WHAT IS NOT DECIDED HERE.** The physical dimensions and what is above what are this issue's; how the label looks is the design's. `align-items: center` stayed, so the two pieces center across the label where they centered down it before, and the room a symbol smaller than the widest leaves — 1.6 mm today — falls below the code. Both are the transposition of what was there rather than a choice, and both are open. **#453 closed the second**: the label is sized for today's symbol alone, so nothing is left below the code to place.

**MEASURED IN A BROWSER, AND THIS TIME A VERSION STEP TOO.** At 1440 and at 375 the cell renders 16.8 x 20.42 mm, the symbol 13.2 mm across a 33-module `viewBox` — 0.4 mm a module — the gap 1.5 mm, the code's line box 2.1167 mm and the code 10.5833 mm wide in Inconsolata, loaded rather than fallen back. The tool item page draws the same 13.2 mm, so #352's equality survives again. **#412 stopped at the version step because the dev origin encodes at version 2 and the session cookie is bound to `localhost`**, so no longer host could be reached signed in. So the page's origin was replaced for the measurement and restored after it, with the diff read back. A 45-character address — a preview host's shape — drew version 3 at 14.8 mm across 37 modules, still 0.4 mm a module, filling the width to the hundredth; a 77-character one encodes at version 4, and the screen named all three tool items as no longer fitting and drew no label. **No print preview was reachable** — `window.print()` returned at once in the browser pane — and no sheet has been printed on paper.

### The label for today's symbol (#453)

The label is **15.2 x 17.32 mm** where it was 16.8 x 20.42, and a Letter sheet holds **143** — 11 across and 13 down — where it held 110. `VERSION_HEADROOM` is **0** where it was 1 and `LABEL_GAP_MM` **0** where it was 1.5 mm; the module is still **0.4 mm** and the code's floor **6 pt**. `LABEL_STOCK` changed two figures, and the grid, the count, the start position's range and the stock's name followed from them; the sheet's refusal and the tool item page's line under the symbol changed their words. **#467 replaced the sheet with a page per label, 11 x 12 mm, and kept the headroom and the gap at 0** — `A label is a page of its own`.

**THE DESIGN'S SMALLER LABEL WAS THIS ONE WITH THREE THINGS TAKEN OUT, AND ADDING THEM BACK UP IS WHAT SHOWED WHICH.** The design drew 15.2 x 17.2 mm and a sheet of 143. The width differs by the headroom alone: 33 modules at 0.4 mm and two insets is 15.2, and the four modules a version step adds are 1.6 mm. The height differs by three things and a rounding: the same 1.6 mm, the 1.5 mm gap, and the code — 6 pt is 2.1167 mm where the design used 2.0, the floor #412 set and #431 replaced — with 0.0033 mm of rounding up on top, and 1.6 + 1.5 + 0.1167 + 0.0033 is the 3.22 between 20.42 and 17.2. Everything else the design used is the code's — the module, the insets, the page, the margin and the die-cut's gap — which is why its 143 is `fitCount`'s answer to its own figures. **So at 6 pt the design's label cannot be the label**: 17.2 mm leaves today's symbol 13.0833 mm of room down against the 13.2 it needs, `symbolBox` refuses it, and 17.32 is the least that carries the code at its floor. Neither drawing became the label as drawn; the design's did, with the code's height.

**THE HEADROOM WENT, AND WHAT IT HAD BEEN PROTECTING WAS NARROWER THAN THE SENTENCE THAT KEPT IT.** #412's reason, carried in the constant's docstring, was that the module sits on its floor, so a version step can only be absorbed by a bigger label, and with no headroom `symbolBox` refuses the symbol and the screen draws no label for that tool item. All of that is still arithmetic. What it left out is what reaches a version step: `offline/tool-label-qr.mjs` holds the path, the code, the error-correction level and the library to version 2, a five-digit daily sequence stays there (#411), and so the one address that steps it is a host longer than seventeen characters. A permanent host moves by somebody's decision, and #340 above already records a move as a reprint of every label glued to a tool — so what one step protected was reprinting onto the same die-cut. The one exception is a move that keeps `hyeusa.com` redirecting, where the old stickers still resolve and only new ones carry the new host; that too is decided at the moment a host is chosen, and it is the condition the docstring names. **What the step cost was permanent: 1.6 mm on the narrow side of every label**, the side #412 found decides whether the sticker goes on a wrench, so a handle between 15.2 and 16.8 mm was refused for it; 33 labels a sheet; and a real stock that had to be 16.8 mm wide where 15.2 now does. **The one host it let print today was a Vercel domain**, which is the host no label may be printed from.

**SO THE HOST'S LENGTH NOW DECIDES WHETHER A LABEL PRINTS, AND THE HOST HAS TO BE SETTLED BEFORE THE FIRST ONE IS.** Version 2 holds 38 characters and the address is 31 at `hyeusa.com`, so a host of up to seventeen characters prints this label. `portal.hyeusa.com` is exactly seventeen, so a subdomain reaches the edge — six letters is the most there is room for — and at that host the room is spent: the thousandth tool item of a day is 39 characters and steps the version. **A permanent host longer than seventeen characters is the condition for putting `VERSION_HEADROOM` back to 1**, and the label then grows 1.6 mm each way, to 16.8 x 18.92 mm and 120 a sheet. #340's rule that no label is printed before the app has its host was a rule about where the stickers point; it is a rule about which stock to buy as well now. **The host is `app.hyeusa.com`, fourteen characters (#467)**, so it prints this label with three to spare; and on tape a label cannot grow, so what `VERSION_HEADROOM`'s docstring names for a step is a wider tape or a print proving thinner margins.

**A VERCEL DOMAIN DRAWS NO SHEET, WHICH IS #411's LOST TELL COMING BACK AS A REFUSAL.** A preview domain's address is 45 characters, version 3, so its symbols do not fit and the sheet names every tool item instead of drawing it. #353 did not refuse a host, on the ground that nothing in the app knows which host is permanent and a guard that guessed would block every local check; this guesses nothing — it is the address's length — and a local server's address is 34 characters and version 2, so every local check still draws. For a host short enough to stay at version 2 the warning above the sheet was still the only defense, which is what `buildToolItemQR`'s docstring said until #454 took the warning off.

**AND IT DOES NOT CLOSE THE MEASUREMENT BOTH FLOORS WAIT ON, WHICH IS WRITTEN DOWN BECAUSE A REFUSED SHEET READS AS THOUGH IT HAD.** `MIN_MODULE_MM` and `MIN_ID_FONT_PT` are citations that reopen only on a sheet printed on paper and read by a phone. A sheet printed from a local server is that sheet: its address is version 2 at the production geometry, so the symbols on it are today's symbols at 0.4 mm a module and the code on it is at its floor. A phone decodes them and cannot open the address, since the host is the laptop that printed it — and the decode is the measurement. So nobody meeting a Vercel domain's empty sheet should conclude that the floors can no longer be settled, and `VERSION_HEADROOM`'s docstring says so where that reader will be. **#467 is that reading, made on the label printer**: both floors are measurements now.

**THE GAP WAS A LAYOUT CHOICE, AND THE LAYOUT IS THE DESIGN'S.** The margin the QR specification asks for is four modules of quiet zone, 1.6 mm here, and the SVG already paints it white inside the symbol's box. With the code's line box starting where that box ends, the ink starts 0.23 mm lower still — #431's measurement of Inconsolata in a one-em line box — so the nearest ink is 1.83 mm from the nearest dark module. Nor was the gap a tolerance: the symbol and the code are one raster printed in one pass, so nothing registers one against the other, and what a die-cut or a feed can miss is `LABEL_SAFE_INSET_MM`'s. The 1.5 mm came in with #353's first layout carrying no source — its docstring said only where it sat — and #431 turned it from beside the symbol to under it. The design drew none, so the code follows the design; **up to 0.78 mm keeps 13 rows**, and from there to 2.46 mm a sheet holds 12, 132 labels.

**TWO SENTENCES WERE TRUE ONLY WHILE A LONG HOST WAS RARE.** The sheet's refusal ended `Larger stock is what fixes it.`, which on a Vercel domain is the wrong advice — nothing there ever fitted, and the fix is printing from the host a label should carry — so it names the host and prescribes nothing. The tool item page drew a symbol that big under `Shown at the size it prints.`, which is false wherever the sheet refuses it; it asks `symbolBox` for `fits` now and says the other line in that case. **Both were already reachable under the headroom**, from a host past forty characters, where an address is version 4 — the shape of a branch preview's — and neither had been seen; what #453 changed is that the main Vercel domain reaches them.

**THREE COMMENTS WERE FALSE AND WERE CORRECTED IN PASSING (#181).** `lib/toolLabelQR.js`' header gave the thousandth tool item of a day as the case against passing the version to the encoder and called it version 3, which stopped being true at #411 — it is 32 characters and version 2, and `offline/tool-label-qr.mjs` asserts exactly that — so the header says what not passing the version buys now: a larger symbol is refused on the sheet by name rather than thrown. `QR_VERSION`'s docstring read the seven characters as a five-digit sequence and a seventeen-character host at once, where they are one or the other or a share of each. And `labels.css` said the symbol's box is sized to `QR_SIDE_MODULES`, which is the defect #353 measured rather than what the sheet does.

**WHAT THE CHECKS HOLD, AND THE HOLE THEY HAD.** The figures are typed out as before. Section 1d of `offline/tool-label-sheet.mjs` is new and reads, off the AST, everything built from the stock: `CELLS_PER_SHEET` as the grid's product, both pitches, the stock's name as a template of its two dimensions, `cellPosition` carrying no number of its own, and the start position's range in the hint, the clamp, the paging and the control's `max`. **Until now each of those passed every figure in the file when pinned to its right value, and the hint and the control's `max` were held by nothing at all** — #412's finding about `LABEL_GRID`, one step downstream. `offline/tool-item-view.mjs` reads the tool item page's box and the line under it. **Eighteen mutations were run and all eighteen fail**; ten of them pin a derived figure to the value it has today, and each of the ten fails only the one assertion added for it.

**MEASURED IN A BROWSER, THE VERSION STEP INCLUDED.** On a dev server at `localhost:3100`, at 1440 and at 375, four tool items of `DEMO Impact Driver` drew cells of 15.20 x 17.32 mm at 10, 27.20, 44.40 and 61.60 mm across, the symbol 13.20 mm over a 33-module `viewBox` — 0.40 mm a module — and the code's line box starting where the symbol's box ends, 2.1167 mm tall and 10.5833 mm wide in Inconsolata, loaded; the stock line read `Stock: 15.2 x 17.32 mm die-cut`. The start position's control held a `max` of 143 and its hint said `1 to 143`; position 12 put the run 29.32 mm down, and 999 clamped to 143, which put the first label in the last cell, at 182 and 241.84 mm, and the other three at the top of a second sheet. Figures a thousandth off those are Chrome's 1/64 px layout unit. The tool item page drew the same 13.20 mm under `Shown at the size it prints.`, so #352's equality holds again. **With the origin of both pages replaced for the measurement, as #431 did, and put back with the diff read back:** a 45-character address — a preview domain's shape — drew no sheet at either width, named all four tool items in the new sentence and left Print disabled, while the tool item page drew 14.80 mm over 37 modules under the new line; a 77-character one drew 16.40 mm over 41, both at 0.40 mm a module. **The label screen is 3 operations and the tool item page 6**, #353's and #352's figures unchanged. The session was minted once, as `soo@`, which is one `Auth Tokens` row and the write that spends it; nothing else was written. **No sheet was printed on paper.**

**WHAT IS NOT DECIDED HERE.** The gap and how the label looks are the design's; the stock is still not bought; and whether a printed sheet scans is the measurement described above, which nobody has made.

### The host off the screen (#454)

The label screen no longer names the host each symbol will encode, and the warning under it went with it. The page still reads the host, because `buildToolItemQR` encodes it, and that is now the one place the reading goes: the host line was the only other, and the warning read nothing, being the same sentence on every host. #340's rule above is what the two stood for, and it is unchanged.

**THE WARNING WENT BECAUSE IT COULD NOT BE TRUE WHERE IT WOULD BE READ.** It showed on every host, since nothing in the app knows which one is permanent, so on the host the app keeps it would be false above every sheet; before then, the reader it would stop is the one who already knows. **The host line went with it for the same reason**: spelled out above the controls, it was the same guard speaking to the same reader, and keeping it alone would have kept the warning's argument with its conclusion taken off.

**THE REFUSAL FOR A HOST TOO LONG FOR THE LABEL KEEPS ITS WORDS, AND WHETHER IT STANDS WITHOUT THE TWO WAS THE ONE QUESTION.** It says `this host`, and its docstring leaned on the host line for what that meant and on the warning for the fix. The host it means is the one the page is served from — the tool item page's line under its symbol has said `this host` the same way since #453 and has never had a host line above it — and the fix was never the sentence's to give: it cannot tell a wrong host from a longer right one, which is why it prescribes nothing. **The design's words for that state were not fitted into it.** They are a mark beside each code and a reason before Print, two places this screen does not have, and a sentence composed from them would be one the design did not draw.

**THE FIGURES, ON A DEV SERVER AGAINST THE LIVE BASE.** The label screen is **3 operations** — the session, one `Tool Items` read and one `Tools` read — #353's figure, since nothing read was removed. **The origin no longer reaches the browser in any form but the symbol's modules**: the page's HTML carries the tool names and the codes in its payload and holds no trace of the origin, which the sheet component was handed as a prop before.

**WHAT A BROWSER SHOWED.** At 1440 and at 375 the four tool items of `DEMO Impact Driver` go from the heading straight to the stock line and draw cells of 15.20 x 17.32 mm at 10, 27.20, 44.40 and 61.60 mm across, the symbol 13.20 mm over a 33-module `viewBox` and the code 10.58 mm wide in Inconsolata, loaded — #453's figures; with every include unchecked there is no sheet, `Nothing is selected, so there is nothing to print.` and a disabled Print; and the address naming nothing is the heading, its sentence and the way back. **With the origin replaced for the measurement and put back with the diff read back, as #431 and #453 did,** a 45-character address drew no sheet at either width, named all four in the refusal and left Print disabled, and nothing on the page named the host. The page is 817 px wide at 375 while a sheet is drawn, the sheet at its true size as the brief records, and 375 without one. The session was the one already open on `localhost`, as `soo@`, and nothing was written to the base.

**WHAT THE CHECKS HOLD.** `offline/tool-label-page.mjs` holds that no word is left for the host or its warning, and reads off the two files' ASTs that the host header is read once, the host only to build the origin and the origin only inside `buildToolItemQR`'s argument, and that the sheet component names neither — beside a planted page that reads the header twice, hands the origin to the sheet and prints the host, seen doing all three. **Eleven mutations were run and all eleven fail the tier, each only on an assertion this issue added**: the three words put back fail the one on the words, and the eight that move the reading — the origin handed to the sheet, taken by it, printed by the page or laundered through a variable, the host printed, the header read a second time, the host line put back under no word of its own, and the builder handed a literal — fail the one on where the host goes. **What it cannot see is a host reaching the screen by another path**: the builder's own `url` carries it too, and a sheet printing that would pass, which the check's comment says.

### A label is a page of its own (#467)

`/tool-items/labels` draws one page per selected label, each the label's size, and the browser's own print dialog sends them to a label printer that takes 12 mm tape and cuts after every page. The label is **11 x 12 mm** — the cut's length across, the tape's width down — with the symbol **9.57 mm** across 33 modules at **0.29 mm**, and the code under it at **5 pt** in Inconsolata, at 400 with no letter-spacing. The sheet went, and its grid, its start position and the positions the start position held open went with it; the include, the sentence for a code the base does not hold, the refusal for a host too long for the label and the cap of a hundred are unchanged, a page per label where they were a cell. `lib/toolLabelPage.js` holds the stock, the arithmetic and the words, `app/(tools)/tool-items/labels/LabelPages.js` draws the pages, and `offline/tool-label-page.mjs` holds the figures by value. **#457 made the screen a dialog**, and `app/(tools)/tool-items/LabelsDialog.js` draws the pages — `The labels as a dialog` below.

**TWO INPUTS FROM THE STOCK, TWO FLOORS FROM A PRINT AND ONE EXTENT FROM THE FACE; THE REST IS ARITHMETIC.** The tape's 12 mm is the label's height, because the page runs across the tape. 11 mm is how much tape one label takes, a round length chosen for the cut. 0.29 mm and 5 pt are the floors the print proved, and the code's ink runs from 0.011 em below its baseline to 0.631 em above it. From those: the symbol is 9.57 mm; the code's height is its ink, 0.642 em of 5 pt, 1.1324 mm; what the tape leaves above and below the two is split evenly, 0.6488 mm; and what 11 mm leaves beside the symbol is 0.715 mm each side. `LABEL_STOCK` holds the stock's two inputs and nothing derived, since a derived figure typed beside its inputs is a second place for one number, and the check fails a third member. **The width does not follow the module**: four equal margins make the label the tape less the code's height, 10.8676 mm, whatever the symbol is, because a larger symbol takes from the margins exactly what it adds to itself.

**WHAT WAS PRINTED AND READ IS 10.87 x 12 mm WITH 0.65 mm ON ALL FOUR SIDES, AND WHAT THIS DRAWS IS 11 x 12.** The label that proved the floors was a PDF made at these values and printed on this label printer, and a phone read it — the measurement both floors rest on. 11 mm was set after it, as a round length for the cut, and it moves the side margins alone, from 0.65 to 0.715 mm; that was judged to need no second print, since the symbol, the code and the margins above and below are the print's. On the office printer a 0.273 mm module and a 5.1 pt code read as well, and they are recorded at the two constants rather than used: they are another printer's, and no smaller module is recorded from this one. A test page of this structure printed by headless Chrome came out as three label-sized pages, all three decoding, at 10.92 x 12.02 mm — the rounding in the next paragraph.

**THE PAGE CHROME AND EDGE PRINT IS NOT QUITE THE LABEL, AND NO WIDTH NEAR 11 mm SURVIVES IT.** Measured in the PDF each saves: a page box is rounded to whole points and then to a three-hundredth of an inch, so 11 x 12 mm comes out 10.922 x 12.023 — 31 points across where 11 mm is 31.18 — while 10.87 mm comes out the same 31 points and 11.2 mm comes out 11.261. Across, the page is 0.078 mm short of the label's face, which comes off the right-hand margin and leaves 0.637 mm there; down, it is 0.023 mm taller than the label. **So 11 mm is a round figure in the code and not, on this evidence, on the tape**: the PDF path hands on 10.922 mm either way, the page the headless test above came out as. Whether a printer's driver rounds the same way is a print's to show rather than a PDF's. `labels.css` records the rounding, and the case it cannot rule out: a page rounded to 34 points down, 11.994 mm, would leave every label 0.006 mm for a page of its own.

**BOTH FLOORS ARE MEASUREMENTS, AND EACH WAS A CITATION UNTIL THIS.** `MIN_MODULE_MM` was 0.4 mm, the figure published as the practical minimum for a phone camera resolving a printed module, and `MIN_ID_FONT_PT` 6 pt, print convention's smallest legible size. #353 named the event that would reopen both — a label printed and read by a phone — and this is that event, so each constant's docstring says what was printed and what read it. **The module is also what the label prints at, but no longer because width is short**: on tape the module moves only the margins, so a larger one would take from margins no print has had and a smaller one would print a symbol not recorded from this printer.

**THE CODE'S HEIGHT IS ITS INK, AND #431's INK FIGURES WERE A CANVAS'S.** From #431 the code's line box was one em, and the label counted room above and below the ink that nothing was printed in, which on tape is margin — so the height is the ink alone. The ink is read out of the face's outlines in the file `next/font` serves: the ten digits and the hyphen run from −11 to 631 of its 1000 units, `8` the tallest and `0`, `3` and `5` the lowest. #431's 0.641 and 0.016 were a canvas's raster bounds of the same glyphs — it reports 0.640625 and 0.015625, measured again — a difference of 0.03 mm on this label.

**THE CODE IS SET BY ITS BASELINE, BECAUSE A CSS LINE BOX PUTS THE BASELINE WHERE THE FACE'S METRICS DO.** Measured in Chromium, a 5 pt line box 0.642 em tall puts the baseline at 0.600 em rather than 0.631, which lifts the ink 0.055 mm into the symbol's box. SVG text's `y` is the baseline itself, so the code is SVG text whose baseline `labelBudget` puts 0.631 em below the symbol's box: the ink starts where the box ends, which is the four modules of quiet zone, 1.16 mm, from the nearest dark module, and `LABEL_GAP_MM` stays 0.

**EACH LABEL IS ONE SVG FROM THE PAGE'S CORNER, BECAUSE A PRINT SNAPS EVERY BOX TO A WHOLE PIXEL.** The first drawing gave the symbol and the code boxes of their own, and on screen both measured where they were put. In the PDF Chrome and Edge save, each box's corner had landed on the nearest CSS pixel — the symbol 0.12 mm above its margin and the code 0.10 mm below — so the margins the print proved came out 0.53 and 0.55 mm instead of 0.65. Nothing inside an SVG is snapped and the page's own corner is a whole pixel on every page, so the symbol and the code are placed in millimeters inside one SVG and only the label as a whole is a box. The check holds a page to that one box with both inside it.

**THE SYMBOL IS FILLED RATHER THAN STROKED, BECAUSE A PRINT DRAWS A STROKE UNDER A POINT AS A HAIRLINE.** `qrcode`'s own SVG draws each run of dark modules as a stroke one module wide, and at 0.29 mm a module is 0.82 pt. Measured on the final layout with only the symbol swapped back to the library's rendering: Chrome and Edge each write the rows at line width 0 and 82% alpha — a hairline — and no page of either PDF decodes at 1200 dpi; with fills, neither PDF carries a hairline state and every page decodes. `lib/toolLabelQR.js` draws the same runs off the same matrix `create` made, as rectangles a module tall, which have no width to round. **#351's reasons for the library's renderer hold of this one**: the margin and the absent `width`/`height` are drawn as it drew them, and there is one drawing of one encoding. What it costs is bytes — about twice the library's, the trade #351 measured at 1,210 against 2,346 — and it saves an encode. Nothing at 0.4 mm a module or larger met this, since that is over a point.

**A VERSION STEP HAS ONLY THE MARGINS TO TAKE FROM, SO THE HEADROOM STAYS 0.** The tape fixes the height and the cut fixes the width, so a version-3 symbol, 37 modules and 10.73 mm, would leave 0.135 mm either side of it and 0.069 mm above and below, against 0.715 and 0.649, and no print has had margins that thin. Such a symbol is named rather than drawn, as since #453. **The host needs no step**: `app.hyeusa.com` is fourteen characters, so the address is 35 and version 2 holds 38, which a six-digit daily sequence fills. Putting `VERSION_HEADROOM` back to 1 would need a tape wide enough to keep today's margins round a version-3 symbol, or a print proving thinner ones.

**A CODE LONGER THAN TEN CHARACTERS HAS ONLY THE SIDE MARGINS, AND NOTHING HAS PRINTED ONE.** Ten characters at 5 pt are 8.82 mm under a 9.57 mm symbol. The thousandth tool item of a day prints eleven, 9.70 mm, which overhangs the symbol 0.07 mm either side and leaves 0.65 mm to each edge — the side margin the print had; twelve leave 0.21 mm and thirteen are wider than the label. The module's header records it, and no day on this base has reached a thousand.

**WHAT WENT WITH THE SHEET, AND WHAT THE SCREEN SAYS INSTEAD.** Seven exports went — `LABEL_GRID`, `CELLS_PER_SHEET`, `LABEL_SAFE_INSET_MM`, whose place the derived margins take, `moduleSizeMm`, `cellPosition`, `readStartPosition` and `paginateLabels` — and the start position's two words and the sheet count's went with them. The stock line reads `Stock: 12 mm tape` and the count `4 labels, one to a page.`, which is what the print dialog's page count has to agree with. Nothing here is a look: the screen is #457's to rebuild as a dialog, and this one carries the labels and the behavior it had. **#457 rebuilt it**, and the count reads `4 labels` beside `11 × 12 mm`.

**WHAT A BROWSER SHOWED, ON A DEV SERVER AGAINST THE LIVE BASE.** At 1440 and at 375, four tool items of `DEMO Impact Driver` drew four pages of 11 x 12 mm — 10.9988 x 11.9993 in Chrome's 1/64 px layout unit — with the symbol at 0.715 and 0.6488 mm from the corner, 9.57 mm over a 33-module `viewBox` with no stroke in it, and the code's baseline 11.3318 mm down, 8.82 mm wide in Inconsolata, loaded. At 375 the page is 375 px wide, where the sheet made it 817. One include unchecked drew three pages and `3 labels, one to a page.`; all four unchecked drew none, `Nothing is selected, so there is nothing to print.` and a disabled Print; a code the base does not hold beside a real one drew the real one's page under the sentence naming it, and on its own drew the sentence, `None of those tools exists.` and the way back. The tool item page drew the same 9.57 mm under `Shown at the size it prints.` **With the origin of both pages replaced for the measurement and put back with the diff read back, as #453 and #454 did,** a 45-character address drew no page at either width, named all four in the refusal and left Print disabled, while the tool item page drew 10.73 mm over 37 modules under its line for a symbol that does not fit. **The label screen is 3 operations and the tool item page 6**, unchanged. The session was the one already open on `localhost`, and nothing was written to the base.

**WHAT THE PDFS SHOWED.** The page as the session rendered it, with its stylesheets and face inlined, was printed by headless Chrome and Edge, since the browser pane cannot open a print dialog. A run of three and a run of four came out as three and four pages in both browsers, every page 10.922 x 12.023 mm, the code's baseline 11.332 mm down at 4.995 pt, the symbol's dark modules starting 1.884 and 1.799 mm from the corner across 7.24 x 7.26 mm, read off a 1200 dpi rendering where 1.875, 1.809 and 7.25 are drawn, and every page decoding at version 2, with no hairline state in either file.

**WHAT THE CHECKS HOLD.** The figures are typed out as before, and `offline/tool-label-page.mjs` reads off the source what no sum can see: that `LABEL_STOCK` holds two inputs, that a page is one SVG with the symbol and the code inside it at the budget's names, that the stylesheet's page size is the stock's, and that the code's face, weight and letter-spacing are the ones the print read. `offline/tool-label-qr.mjs` holds the fills, the decode of the drawing itself and the extent of its dark modules, and encodes the production address at `app.hyeusa.com`. **Thirty-six mutations were run and all thirty-six fail the tier**; three of them — every symbol sized from its own budget, the runs drawn without the quiet zone, and the tool item page handing `symbolBox` no budget — pass every figure and fail only on an assertion this issue added.

### The labels as a dialog (#457)

The labels are a dialog over the page that opens them, and `/tool-items/labels` is gone. `app/(tools)/tool-items/LabelsDialog.js` is the dialog and its opener — on a tool's page for its selection, on a tool item's page for its own label — and `readToolItemLabelsAction` beside it in `app/(tools)/tool-items/actions.js` reads a tool's run; `labels.css` moved beside them. The frame is #456's `DialogFrame`, given a second build for a preview, and `lib/toolLabelPage.js` gained the dialog's words, `LABEL_PREVIEW_SCALE` and `describeLabelRun`, which is what a run prints of what it named. `offline/tool-label-page.mjs`, `offline/dialog-frame.mjs`, `offline/tool-list-view.mjs` and `offline/tool-item-view.mjs` hold them.

**EACH OPENER HANDS OVER WHAT ITS PAGE HAS, AND ONLY A TOOL'S PAGE READS THE BASE.** A tool's page holds its selection as printed ids off its own address and never asks the base about them (#443), and a symbol can be built only on the server, since `lib/toolLabelQR.js` imports `qrcode` (#351, #353) — so its press reads the run: the session and one `Tool Items` query per 50, each symbol built from the origin and the id. Drawing from the ids alone was the other answer, and it draws a sticker for a code no tool item carries, a label pointing at nothing; the read names those instead. A tool item's page has read its record and built its symbol as it rendered, so it hands the dialog that one label and the press opens at once — `buildToolItemLabel` builds the symbol and the code once for both.

**NOTHING IS WRITTEN TO THE ADDRESS, WHICH IS #456'S SHAPE RATHER THAN #459'S.** A tool's page already carries the selection, so a copied link is a request to print those labels again and its reader is one press from them; an open dialog on the address would open a print dialog for whoever followed the link, and read the run on every arrival. #459's dialogs ride on the address because they account for something that happened and must outlast a reload until answered, and a print has not happened yet. No redirect stands at the old address, for #456's reason: nothing printed carries it, and nothing was deployed.

**THE PRINT KEEPS THE PAGES AND TAKES EVERYTHING ELSE OFF THE PAPER.** A modal dialog is in the top layer over a backdrop, its pages in a pane that scrolls; printed as it stands, the page behind it would print, the frame over it, and the pane would cut the run at its own height. So while a dialog holding label pages is open, everything that neither holds it nor is in it is not drawn, everything holding it draws no box, and the dialog sits at the first page's corner with no room, edge or shadow — the top layer cannot be made static, and Chrome and Edge carry what it holds from page to page; its backdrop, its head and its column are not drawn, and the pane neither scrolls nor clips. **The page name has to be the root's, measured in probes both browsers printed**: given to the label pages, both printed every page at Letter, since the pages are in the top layer, which no page name is taken from; given to the root while the dialog is open, one page per label at the label's size, and with the dialog shut the page at Letter. **And the page is #467's one drawing**: the pane draws it at twice its size, 1i's 22 × 24 mm, by a zoom the print takes back to 1, so nothing inside a label moves between the two and neither of #467's defects can come back — the PDFs below are its figures.

**THREE STATES, AND A LONG HOST BORROWS THE THIRD.** 1i draws a run with every code on record, one with some not, and one with none: `4 labels`; `2 of 4 labels` with the codes under `2 codes not found`; and `No labels to print.` in the pane beside `0 of 3 labels`, the codes and the commitment disabled saying the title's words, with no steps. The screen's other states cannot arise in the dialog. A tool's page refuses an empty selection and one past a hundred before a press (#443), so the read throws on a longer run, which only a caller with no page can send; an address naming nothing, and `None of those tools exists.`, went with the address. **A host too long for the symbols is the one state the code keeps that 1i does not draw**: it takes the third state's shape with the host's sentence where the codes stand, and draws no page.

**A PRESS DURING THE READ IS IGNORED RATHER THAN THE OPENER DISABLED, WHICH A BROWSER SHOWED.** Disabled while the read was in flight, the opener dropped focus to the page under the pointer, so the dialog opened with nothing to hand focus back to, and closing it left focus on `BODY` where CLAUDE.md's overlay rule wants the opener. 1i draws nothing between the press and the dialog, and #469's submitting state is a dialog's action, which this read is not.

**A RUN NAMING ANOTHER TOOL'S TOOL ITEMS PRINTS UNDER THIS TOOL'S NAME, SEEN WITH A TYPED ADDRESS.** The line under the title is the opening page's tool, and only an address somebody typed can put another tool's tool item in a tool's selection: a hundred typed ids on `DEMO Impact Driver`, a tool of four, opened `18 of 100 labels`, fourteen of them another tool's. The labels are right, each carrying its own code, and only the line is wrong for them; the screen named each tool item by its tool until this issue, and the dialog's header records it.

**THE FIGURES, ON A DEV SERVER AGAINST THE LIVE BASE.** A tool's page is **4 operations**, #339's figure, and a tool item's **6**, #352's: neither reads anything for the dialog. `readToolItemLabelsAction` is **2** — the session and one `Tool Items` read — for runs of four and of forty, and **3** for a hundred; `/tool-items/labels` was 3 for either, its third read being the `Tools` its picker named each tool item by.

**WHAT A BROWSER SHOWED.** As the session already open on `localhost`, at 1440 and 375:

- **Against 1i**: 780 × 520, the pane 440 and the column 340; the title at 464, 24, the line under it at 50 and the close at 732, 22; the count at 90, the steps' heading at 134 and the steps at 162 and 190; Cancel and Print at 551 and 639, 460 down; a page 22 × 24 mm, its symbol 19.14 mm, and the code in Inconsolata at 400. With some codes not on record, their heading at 134 and the codes at 162 and 186, the steps under them from 230; with none, `No labels to print.` centered in the pane and the commitment at Blue's 40%. Each gap between the count's words is 1.3 px narrower than 1i's, whose count is spans held apart by 4 of padding where the app's is one sentence with its spaces.
- **The phone**: at 375 the dialog is 319 wide and stacks the head, the pane and the column, with two labels across the pane and nothing scrolling sideways.
- **The openers**: from a tool's page on its selection, and from a tool item's on its own label, a retired one's included. A tool's opener stood disabled beside `Nothing is selected, so there is nothing to print.` and beside `101 selected, and one print takes at most 100.`, and forty selected across both pages of `DEMO Angle Grinder` opened `40 labels`.
- **A long host**: with the origin of both pages replaced for the measurement and put back with the diff read back, as #431, #453 and #454 did, the tool item page opened `0 of 1 label` and a tool's `0 of 3 labels`, each with the host's sentence, no page and the commitment disabled.
- **Closing**: Escape, the close and Cancel each closed it and gave focus back to the opener; a press of the backdrop did nothing and the address did not move. With `window.print` stubbed, Print printed with the face loaded and the dialog open, and the dialog closed on `afterprint` with focus back on the opener.
- **Over #460's rail**, once it had landed: the dialog opened at the same 780 × 520 in the middle of the window, its backdrop over the rail as well.
- **In a pane that draws no frames**, the column's Rules wait for one, as #456 found: the hundred typed ids' eighty-two codes not found scrolled the column, and its Rules came on the first scroll event.

**WHAT THE PDFS SHOWED.** The page as the session rendered it, with the dialog open and its stylesheets and faces inlined, was printed by headless Chrome and Edge, as #467 did. Runs of one, two, four and forty came out as that many pages in both, every page 10.922 x 12.023 mm, carrying its own code and nothing else in Inconsolata, the baseline 11.332 mm down at 4.995 pt and the symbol's dark modules from 1.884 and 1.799 mm across 7.24 x 7.26 mm at 1200 dpi — #467's figures — and each page decoding at version 2 to its own address, with no stroke and no hairline state in either file. With the dialog shut, and with one open that prints nothing, the page printed at Letter. **Printed again under #460's rail frame**, once it had landed: four labels from a tool's page and one from a tool item's came out as four pages and one in both browsers, every page as above and each decoding to its own address, with the rail and the breadcrumb off the paper; with the dialog shut, the tool's page printed at Letter without its rail. **Not seen**: a print from the browser's own print dialog, which the pane cannot open, and a label off the tape from the dialog.

**NOTHING WAS WRITTEN TO THE BASE.**

**WHAT THE CHECKS HOLD.** `offline/tool-label-page.mjs` was rewritten for the dialog: by value, what `describeLabelRun` makes of each state and every word; off the source, the action reading its ids through `readToolItemIds`, throwing past the cap and naming a code it did not find through `namedCode`, the stylesheet's named page and each rule taking the rest off the paper, the pane's tree from list to page with no text in it, the one drawing zoomed by the dialog's scale on screen and by 1 at print, the frame handed the pages as its preview, each part of the column under its own test, the press waiting for the face and closing on `afterprint`, and the opener disabled by its page's reason alone. `offline/dialog-frame.mjs` holds the frame's second build and the body taking the column's spare room, `offline/tool-list-view.mjs` and `offline/tool-item-view.mjs` what each opener hands the dialog, and `offline/screen-briefs.mjs` the dialog's brief to its two pages. **Forty-three mutations were run, and forty failed the tier at first.** Three survived, each a check's: the frame handed no preview, which draws no pane at all; a tool's opener given the tool item's word; and the body taking no spare room, which lifts the actions off the column's foot. Each has an assertion now, and all forty-three fail the tier.

## The first transition, and the confirmation that is one press (#362)

`/tool-items/[toolItemId]` offers the one transition its status allows, and confirming it writes a `Tool Log` row and moves the two cached fields. `lib/toolStatus.js` gained the map from a status to what it offers, `lib/toolTransition.js` is the screen's rule and its words, `recordToolItemEventAction` is the write, and `offline/tool-transition.mjs` holds all of it.

**THE CONFIRMATION IS THAT ARRIVING IS NOT ACTING, AND CLAUDE.md ALREADY DECIDED THE SHAPE.** Every confirmation in this app is a modal — the two withdrawals, the two deletions, the overage and the direct purchase — and the rule beside them says why: *a modal is for an act that cannot be undone; an act that can is edited in place* (#318). A check-out CAN be undone, by the check-in that is the very next thing this screen offers, which is `lib/airtable/toolLog.js`'s own doctrine that correcting a mistaken scan is another row. So the issue's requirement — the scan changes nothing, the transition takes a confirmation — is met by the page: a QR opens it, it states the printed id and the status, and pressing is the act. **A mis-scan cannot flip anything**; a mis-scan followed by a mis-press can, and what catches that is the printed id at the top of the page, which is the string on the sticker in the reader's hand. A modal would restate it. **#459 dropped the rule this paragraph applies** — CLAUDE.md no longer says where a modal goes, and the design does — so the shape stood on this reading until #458 put the transitions in the dialogs the design draws for them. The reading survived there: the dialog a check-out opens asks for the job and the name its row needs rather than confirming, and a check-in on one job, which needs neither, is still this one press (`The transitions and the retirement as dialogs`).

**AND THE COST OF THE OTHER ANSWER IS WHAT MAKES THIS MORE THAN A RULE LOOKUP.** A project starts with a manager scanning their job's tools out one at a time and ends with them scanning back what returned, so a transition is dozens a day where a deletion is a handful a year. A second tap on every one of them is the kind of friction people route around, and the way they route around it is a note on paper and a typing session that never happens — at which point the app holds nothing. **#363 IS THE OTHER SIDE OF THE SAME SPLIT AND CONFIRMS IT INDEPENDENTLY**: retiring cannot be undone, and that issue's own body says its required reason is what stands between a mis-tap and a state the app cannot leave. The weight belongs on the irreversible transition, and this is not one.

**WHAT FOLLOWS A TRANSITION IS THE PAGE ITSELF, AND NOTHING SAYS SO TWICE (#321).** The action redirects to the address it came from — the shape the signing-chain actions already have — so the status flips, an entry appears at the foot of the history — at its head since #463, which drew the history newest first — and the control now reads the other way. That is the whole confirmation. **The next act on this screen is opening the camera again**, which is the argument against a banner rather than merely for the rule: anything that has to be read or dismissed stands between the reader and the next tool. **This sentence went on to say that the app carries no `revalidatePath` anywhere, which stayed true and stopped meaning anything**: the clause was there to say that a redirect is how freshness is obtained here, and #378 is where a refusal stopped obtaining it that way — see that issue's section below.

**THE JOB IS #338's RULE UNCHANGED, AND THE TWO SHAPES ARE ONE FORM.** One assignment is stated and used without asking, several are chosen from, nothing types one, and somebody with none is refused. The form branches on the count in the one place the registration form branches, so the two cases are not two screens and a person on one job never meets a picker. **#458 follows 0l instead, as #456's registration had**: at a desk one assignment is a choice already made, and below the phone's edge the job is stated with no sheet to open.

**WHERE THAT PUTS TWO `Job` VALUES ON ONE SCREEN, AND WHY THE ANSWER IS A SENTENCE RATHER THAN A SECOND WORD.** The header states where the tool item was last scanned; the control states where this event will be recorded. Both are `Job` because both are the same fact — the job an event happened on — so coining a second label would be two words for one thing. When they differ, the difference is not a labeling problem: it is a tool that has been carried to another site, which the issue says is recorded as it happened rather than refused. So the screen says so, in one line that appears only then. Measured in a browser: `scoped-fixture@`, assigned to `26-DEMO-01`, opening a tool item last scanned on `26-DEMO-02` sees it with no picker at all, and `soo@` sees it appear and disappear as the picker moves between the two. **#463's foot bar draws no such line**: 1f gives the phone's transition the pill alone, stating the one job the event goes on, and the desk's dialog is where the line stands.

**`Retired` IS BUILT NOW AND CANNOT BE REACHED UNTIL #363, WHICH IS A DIFFERENT CASE FROM THE COMPARISON #340 REFUSED TO BUILD.** That one was unreachable by any path and would have stayed so; this one has an issue filed against it. Three things force it anyway. `EVENT_OFFERED_BY_STATUS` has to be TOTAL over the status vocabulary or a fourth status inherits an answer in silence — which is `summarizeTools`' reason for drawing all three counts. The ACTION has to refuse it whatever the screen shows, because a Server Action is directly callable. And the page has to render something for a `Retired` tool item, whose page is reachable — #351 records that one still gets a symbol — and the alternative to a sentence is a control that lies. **What #363 inherits is therefore complete**: the `null` entry, the refusal, and the sentence built from the status rather than naming `Retired`. That issue adds the writer and is the first reader of any of it. **The sentence names no status since #463**: the design's `Nothing more can be recorded here.` states the end with the status above it, and a desk draws nothing in the controls' place for a retired tool at all.

**NO LOCK, AND THAT IS FORCED RATHER THAN JUDGED.** Wrapping the action in `withKeyLock` on the tool item would nest with the per-parent lock `generateChildId` takes inside `createToolLogEntry`, which CLAUDE.md's concurrency section forbids and `lib/materialsCache.js` is the precedent against. It would also buy little, for `withKeyLock`'s standing reason: two concurrent Vercel invocations read the same status either way.

**WHAT IS CLOSED INSTEAD IS THE HALF WITH A PERSON BEHIND IT.** The form submits the event it was OFFERED; the action re-derives the event from the stored status and refuses if the two disagree. That costs nothing — the action reads the tool item anyway — and it stops the failure that would otherwise be silent and backwards: a page left open while somebody else moved the tool, whose button says `Check out` while the tool item is already `Out`, recording a check-in. Measured in two browser tabs: the stale tab's press is refused, names the status the base holds, and writes no row. **WHAT IS NOT CLOSED, UNOBSERVED AND RECORDED IN THE ACTION'S HEADER:** two people scanning one physical tool at the same instant can both confirm, both rows land — which is correct for an append-only log — and the cache takes whichever update lands last. One more scan repairs it.

**AND #340's CLAIM THAT A CACHE/LOG DISAGREEMENT IS UNREACHABLE IS NOW FALSE, WHICH THIS ISSUE CORRECTS RATHER THAN LEAVES.** It said the state needed a hand edit in Airtable, which this base forbids. This action writes the log row and then the cache, two writes with no transaction between them, so a failure in the second reaches it by an app path with nobody touching the base. **The conclusion survives and the reason is that only one of #340's four grounds was the unreachability.** The other three stand: a screen sentence would need a reader who knows what a cache is, a design for where it sits, a brief entry and a check. In place of a comparison, three things carry it. The action NAMES the state to the one person who can act on it and logs the record id, never writing to Airtable, which is `lib/rollbackReport.js`'s rule for a failed restore. The next press repairs it, in both directions — the stale status offers a transition, that event is written again, and the cache lands in step; the cost is a duplicate log row, which is what an append-only log already pays for a mistaken scan. And deriving the status on THIS page would still be wrong on its own terms, because the two list screens read the cache and this page would become the one surface saying something different about where a tool item is.

**THE LOG IS WRITTEN FIRST AND NOTHING ROLLS BACK.** A cache written with no row behind it loses the event with nowhere else holding it; a row with a stale cache is recoverable and is reported. `Tool Log` has no delete, so undoing the first write is not available even in principle — the same posture #338 takes toward a tool item whose `Created` row was not written.

**`assignedJobsFor` STAYS IN `lib/toolRegistration.js` AND THE NAME IS NOW NARROWER THAN THE CONTENTS.** It is the tools axis's rule for which job an actor may file an event against, not a fact about registering anything, and this issue is its second consumer. Moving it would cost a module of its own plus a line in CLAUDE.md's service-layer list — in an issue that already trims two lines to pay for one — and it would not take `noJob` with it, since that sentence names the act and the two acts differ. `naming.md`'s test settles it: narrow is not wrong. **The condition for moving is a third caller and #363 is it** — retiring writes a `Tool Log` row and every row carries a job from the actor — which is `countsAsOrdered`'s mechanism exactly: it sat in `lib/materialPriceView.js` with the condition written down until #169 was the third consumer. That issue inherits a trigger that has already fired.

**THE FIGURES, MEASURED ON A DEV SERVER AGAINST THE LIVE BASE.** The page is **6 operations and unchanged** — the session and the whole job list are already read for the history's names, so the offer costs nothing, and 6 is the figure #352 recorded for a tool item with one log row. Confirmed at one log row and at four. The action is **7**: the session, the tool item, the job list, three for the log row (`generateChildId`'s parent find, the sibling read that finds the highest sequence, and the create) and one for the cache. **A refusal is 3** and writes nothing — the session, the tool item and the job list are what the verdict is reached from. Six rather than seven on a tool item with no history at all, because `findChildRecords` on an empty link array costs nothing; that is #338's unlogged state and it is why the figure is the ordinary case rather than a law.

**THE TWO LIST SCREENS HAVE NOW SEEN THE STATE THEY HAD NEVER SEEN.** #339 recorded that `Out` and `Retired` had never been rendered with a figure in them. `/tools` reads `DEMO Impact Driver — In Stock 3, Out 1, Retired 0`, and that tool's own page carries a `Status` column holding two values. Both are left standing: reverting would mean writing a check-in that describes an event that did not happen on a site, which is exactly what this file refuses for a late `Created` row. `Retired` was still unrendered when this was written and #363 is where it stopped being.

**WHAT THE CHECK HOLDS, AND THE PART OF IT THAT IS NOT A PURE FUNCTION.** The offer, the two refusals and their order, the submission rules and the move notice are ordinary assertions. The rest is read off the AST, because naming the functions the action must call is satisfied by every wrong version of the call: `event`, `formData.get("event")` and `"Checked Out"` are all arguments to `createToolLogEntry`, and only the ARGUMENT tells them apart. That is #352's answer to the trap #351 and #353 each fell into once. **One assertion had to be moved from behavior to source for a reason worth keeping**: `readSubmission` returns only when the submitted event equals the plan's, so handing back the submitted one is behaviorally identical — measured by mutation, which passed 81 of 81 — and it stops being identical the moment somebody loosens the comparison. **Thirteen mutations were run and all thirteen fail the tier**, including two the first version missed: a redirect moved into a `finally` (an ancestry test asks about the try BLOCK, so the position is asserted as well) and that same `readSubmission` return.

## Retiring a tool item, and the rule that went with the field (#363)

The tool item's page offers a second control beside the transition — an item in the `More actions` menu since #463 — and confirming it in a modal writes a `Retired` row and moves the two cached fields. `lib/toolStatus.js` gained the third map, `lib/toolTransition.js` widened to hold both transitions, `retireToolItemAction` is the write, and `offline/tool-transition.mjs` widened with the module. Nothing new was needed on the page, in the route table or in the copy arrangement — #362 built all of it.

**THE MODAL IS WHERE #362's OWN ARGUMENT LANDS ON THE OTHER SIDE.** That issue took CLAUDE.md's rule — a modal is for an act that cannot be undone; an act that can is edited in place — and read it as one press, because a check-out is undone by the check-in the same control offers a moment later. Nothing undoes this one. The frequency runs the same way and doubles the answer: a check-out is dozens a day, so a second tap is friction people route around and the routing-around is a note on paper; a tool item is retired once, ever, so a heavier confirmation costs nothing anybody meets twice. **#459 dropped the rule this starts from**, and the retirement's dialog stands on the design's drawing of it and on the frequency argument; #458 moved it onto the design's frame, as 0l's Confirm.

**AND WHAT THE MODAL IS FOR IS THE SENTENCE, NOT THE EXTRA TAP.** `docs/briefs/_shared.md` says it of the three deletion voices — they are accurate accounts of what becomes true rather than warnings, and that voice is the point of a confirmation. A person who opened this by accident meets three facts before the button: it stops counting as something the company holds, nothing more can be recorded against it, and its row and its whole history stay. Then the app's one ending, `This cannot be undone.` **#458 took the design's sentence in its place**, which says the first two and its own ending, `This can't be undone.` — the form `lib/poWithdraw.js` already ends on.

**THE REQUIRED REASON WAS RETIRED RATHER THAN IMPLEMENTED, AND THE ARGUMENT IS THAT ITS GROUNDS WENT ONE AT A TIME.** #334 recorded the rule against `Lost` and `Retired` together, on two grounds: a record asking who was responsible, and the act being irreversible. #335 deleted `Lost`, and the first ground went with it — nobody reports an individual tool item missing, so there is no responsibility to record. The second ground is real and the modal above is what bears it: what stands between a mis-tap and a state the app cannot leave is a control that acts on nothing, a sentence saying what becomes true, and a second deliberate press. With both grounds accounted for there was nothing left holding the rule up, so the field it was to live in had no remaining purpose.

**SO `Tool Log."Notes"` COMES OFF THE BASE, AND THE REMOVAL IS SAFE FOR THREE MEASURED REASONS.** 25 rows, none of them holding a value; no formula, rollup, lookup or view anywhere on the base referencing the field id; and one default view on the table. **The Metadata API offers CREATE and UPDATE for a field and no DELETE** — `airtable-access.md` records that from #335 and this issue re-measured it, 404 against the real field id with the field still standing afterwards — so **the deletion is a hand step in the Airtable UI and the pull request names it as one.** Nothing in the repository reads or writes the field from this commit onward, which is what makes the order safe either way: an unread field is inert, and a field read after deletion would have been the quiet symptom `airtable-access.md` warns about.

**WHAT WENT WITH THE FIELD, IN THE SAME COMMIT.** The `Notes` pair in `logRowFacts` and the drops-when-blank rule around it; `TOOL_ITEM_COPY.notesLabel`; the `notes` parameter of `createToolLogEntry` and the value in its mapper; the assertions in `offline/tool-item-view.mjs` that held the rule, replaced by the opposite claim — four is the whole list and a value handed in beside them does not reach the screen; the brief's entry for a note on a history entry; and this file's own `Open — decided, not yet enforceable` section, whose single subject this was.

**AND `logRowFacts` KEEPS ITS MODULE, WHICH IS WORTH THE PARAGRAPH BECAUSE THE QUESTION WAS ASKED.** Losing the drop rule makes it shorter, not rule-free: it still fixes the ORDER and the four-ness, which the brief calls invariants of the base. `lib/toolItemView.js` was never only that function either — it owns `TOOL_ITEM_COPY`, which `lib/toolListView.js` and `lib/toolTransition.js` both read out of rather than re-spelling, and `EVENT_AT_FORMAT` with its measured merge condition. **The decisive half is structural:** the page imports `lib/airtable/`, so a rule moved into it leaves the offline tier altogether, which is the same reason `lib/toolStatus.js` was split out in the first place.

**`Retired` IS OFFERED FROM `Out` AS WELL AS FROM `In stock`, AND REFUSING IT WOULD MANUFACTURE A LIE.** A tool that broke on a site is retired from there; requiring a check-in first would put an event in the log that did not happen, which is the argument #341 made about reassignment before that issue closed. The rule is `MAY_RETIRE_FROM_STATUS`, a third map beside the two #362 left. **It is not `!eventOfferedBy(status)` and the coincidence is exactly why:** that map answers what a SCAN offers and this one what a person DESIGNATES — `TOOL_EVENT`'s own docstring already splits the two — and they agree on all three of today's statuses. A fourth that offered no scan and could still be retired would silently lose its control. The check asserts the agreement value by value rather than deriving either side from the other.

**TWO CONTROLS ON ONE SCREEN, AND WHAT KEEPS THEM FROM READING ALIKE IS NOT STYLING.** The transition SUBMITS — pressing it records — and the retirement OPENS, so pressing it acts on nothing. That is a difference in kind that survives any palette. The words carry the other half: `Check out` names no object where `Retire this tool item` does, which is `Withdraw this PO`'s shape one axis over. What the design is free to do is everything else, and `docs/briefs/tool-items-toolItemId.md` says which half is which. **The design added a third in #463**: the retirement is a menu item behind an icon, the transition the filled button the header and the foot bar end on.

**THIS AXIS'S MODAL IS THE SECOND IN THE APP TO HONOR THE KEYBOARD RULE, WHICH MEANS THERE ARE NOW TWO KINDS OF MODAL.** CLAUDE.md requires anything opening over the page to close on `Escape` as well as by its opener and to hand focus back; `app/components/FileViewer.js` was the first and its header records that the other ten do not. This one does — `Escape`, focus return, focus into the card, `role="dialog"` — and the ten are untouched, because repairing them is not this issue. **The inconsistency is recorded rather than left to be discovered:** `docs/briefs/_shared.md` carries it beside the modal-styling constraint, which is where the next pass over modals will meet it, and the component's own header says the same thing to whoever edits it.

**NO REASON MEANS NO SECOND WORD FOR ONE FIELD, WHICH WAS THE OTHER THING THAT WENT.** A modal asking for a `Reason` while the history rendered `Notes` would have been one field under two screen words on one screen, and the divergence would have needed a row in `naming.md`. Both disappeared with the field.

**THE RETIREMENT ASKED FOR A JOB AND NO LONGER DOES, WHICH IS THE ONE DECISION THIS ISSUE MADE TWICE.** The first shape followed the enumerated rule — registration, check-out and check-in take the actor's job, so the retirement did too — and put a picker in the modal beside the transition form's. That is two pickers on one screen asking one question, free to disagree, which is how the defect was found; and the disagreement is what showed the rule was wrong rather than merely repeated. The section above carries the corrected rule and the row that proved it. What changed here: the modal takes no input at all, the page asks the question once, and `readRetirement` hands back the tool item's own job rather than validating a submitted one.

**AND THE ROW THE DEFECT WROTE WAS REPAIRED RATHER THAN LEFT, WHICH IS WORTH THE PARAGRAPH BECAUSE `Tool Log` IS APPEND-ONLY.** `HYE-TL-260909-004-006` carried `26-DEMO-01` where the tool item had last been scanned into `26-DEMO-02`, and `Tool Items."Job"` carried the same wrong value. Two record PATCHes set both to `26-DEMO-02` and touched nothing else. **The append-only rule bars rewriting what was true at a moment, and what was true at that moment is `26-DEMO-02`** — `26-DEMO-01` is what the defect wrote, so repairing it is not editing history. A compensating row was not available either: `Retired` is terminal by the very rule this issue adds, so nothing may be appended after it. The row was written while verifying an unmerged branch and no screen had shown it to anybody. **The general shape, for the next time:** a value a defect wrote may be corrected in the same commit that fixes the defect; an event somebody really performed may not.

**`assignedJobsFor` MOVED, AND THE CONDITION #362 WROTE DOWN IS WHAT MOVED IT.** That issue left it in `lib/toolRegistration.js` under a name narrower than its contents and recorded the trigger: a third caller.
